### [1] [Fourier Transform-Based Computation of Two-Electron Four-Center Coulomb Repulsion Integrals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12608)
*William Chen,Lior Silberman,Ury Segal,Chen Greif*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A Fourier-based method to evaluate two-electron four-center Coulomb repulsion integrals over Slater-type orbitals, reducing the problem to 1D radial quadrature via precomputed spherical Fourier-Bessel coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-electron four-center Coulomb repulsion integrals over Slater-type orbitals are computationally expensive; an efficient and accurate evaluation scheme is needed.

Method: Spherical Fourier-Bessel coefficients of the convolution factors are precomputed and reused; the spherical part of the integral is evaluated directly, leaving a one-dimensional radial quadrature problem.

Result: For ten reference integrals, the largest absolute discrepancy is 1.7e-9. A preliminary resident-cache benchmark computes ~4.2e5 unique integrals per second on an NVIDIA V100 at the contraction stage.

Conclusion: The method is accurate and shows promising GPU performance for Slater-type orbital integral evaluation.

Abstract: We present a Fourier-based method for evaluating two-electron four-center Coulomb repulsion integrals over Slater-type orbitals. Spherical Fourier-Bessel coefficients of the convolution factors are precomputed and reused. Since the spherical part of the integral can be evaluated directly, there remains only a one-dimensional quadrature problem in the radial direction. For the ten reference integrals reported here, the largest absolute discrepancy is $1.7\times10^{-9}$. A preliminary resident-cache benchmark computes approximately $4.2\times10^5$ unique integrals per second on an NVIDIA V100 at the contraction stage.

</details>


### [2] [Median randomized quasi-Monte Carlo for nested integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12643)
*Arved Bartuska,Yang Liu,Raúl Tempone*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper extends median randomized quasi-Monte Carlo (mRQMC) to nested integrals, proposing a double-loop mRQMC estimator with super-polynomial convergence when both integrands are analytic, and adds an importance-sampling regularization to handle irregular outer integrands in expected information gain (EIG) applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nested integrals arise in Bayesian experimental design, medical decision making, and computational finance, and are more demanding than standard integrals. Existing nested estimators (double-loop, multilevel Monte Carlo) only achieve algebraic convergence. mRQMC gives super-polynomial rates for analytic integrands, motivating its extension to nested integration.

Method: A novel double-loop mRQMC estimator for nested integrals, achieving super-polynomial convergence if both inner and outer integrands are analytic. Since EIG outer integrands lack regularity, a theoretically-grounded importance sampling regularization scheme is introduced.

Result: Super-polynomial rates hold for the inner integral; for the outer integral in EIG only an algebraic rate α+3/2-ε (α = order of mixed derivatives of the regularized outer integrand) is guaranteed. Experiments show substantial gains over sample-mean double-loop RQMC in moderately high dimensions for a polynomial example, and improved convergence for EIG estimation.

Conclusion: Double-loop mRQMC with importance sampling regularization offers faster convergence than standard nested Monte Carlo methods, with practical benefits demonstrated for polynomial examples and the challenging EIG estimation setting.

Abstract: Nested integrals $\int f\left(\int g(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}{}\boldsymbol{x}\right)\mathrm{d}{}\boldsymbol{y}$ where $\boldsymbol{y}\in[0,1]^{d_1}$ and $\boldsymbol{x}\in[0,1]^{d_2}$ frequently arise in Bayesian experimental design, medical decision making, and computational finance. Statistical estimators for nested integrals are more computationally demanding than for standard integrals. Recent studies demonstrate that the median randomized quasi-Monte Carlo (mRQMC) method achieves super-polynomial convergence rates for analytic integrands. We extend mRQMC methods to nested integrations and propose a novel double-loop mRQMC estimator with super-polynomial convergence rate if both the inner and outer integrands are analytic. Established estimators for nested integrals, such as double-loop or multilevel Monte Carlo, are by contrast limited to algebraic convergence rates. However, expected information gain (EIG) applications from experimental design typically do not fulfill this condition due to a lack of regularity in the outer integrand. We address this through a theoretically-grounded importance sampling regularization scheme. Although super-polynomial convergence rates remain achievable for the inner integral, only the algebraic rate $α+3/2-ε$ for any $ε>0$ can be theoretically guaranteed for the outer integral in the EIG, where $α\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}$ denotes the order of the mixed derivatives of the regularized outer integrand. Our approach shows substantial improvements over double-loop randomized quasi-Monte Carlo methods based on the sample mean in moderately high-dimensional settings for a simple polynomial example. Moreover, we demonstrate improved convergence behavior of the median-based estimator for the more challenging setting of EIG estimation.

</details>


### [3] [Variational neural transport for aggregation-diffusion equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12745)
*Qianyi Li,Hailiang Liu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A neural transport framework solves nonlinear nonlocal aggregation-diffusion equations by recasting them as a constrained optimal control problem via the Onsager variational principle, with a neural network velocity field and rigorous error analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aggregation-diffusion equations (chemotaxis, polymer dynamics) are nonlinear and nonlocal, making them challenging to solve. The authors seek a flexible, accurate neural approach grounded in a variational principle.

Method: Using the Onsager variational principle, the equation is reformulated as a constrained optimal control problem with the velocity field as control and density evolving via the continuity equation. The velocity is parameterized by a neural network trained on the variational objective along particle trajectories. Density and generalized score equations are coupled into a single system solved with the Euler method, enabling efficient score propagation for loss evaluation.

Result: Rigorous error analysis decomposes total error into approximation, optimization, Monte Carlo sampling, and time-discretization components, with proven error bounds. Numerical experiments on representative models show accuracy and effectiveness.

Conclusion: The neural transport framework offers an accurate, theoretically grounded method for nonlinear, nonlocal aggregation-diffusion equations, supported by error bounds and empirical validation.

Abstract: We develop a neural transport framework for nonlinear, nonlocal aggregationdiffusion equations arising in chemotaxis, polymer dynamics, and related applications. Based on the Onsager variational principle, we reformulate the governing equation as a constrained optimal control problem, taking the velocity field as the control and evolving the density via the associated continuity equation. The velocity field is parameterized by a neural network trained on the variational objective evaluated along particle trajectories. By coupling the density and generalized score equations into a single system solved via the Euler method, the score function propagates efficiently for loss evaluation. Furthermore, we establish a rigorous error analysis that decomposes the total error into approximation, optimization, Monte Carlo sampling, and time-discretization components, and prove corresponding error bounds. Numerical experiments across several representative models demonstrate the accuracy and effectiveness of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [4] [A kernel-independent precorrected-FFT method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12821)
*Tristan Goodwill,Owen Melia*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The precorrected-FFT method is extended (via improved proxy points) to efficiently compute N-body sums for arbitrary kernels, requiring only pointwise kernel access. The pcfft package enables favorable scaling versus other kernel-independent fast summation methods, with applications in scattering, geophysics, and microbial community formation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precorrected-FFT has been a fast method for N-body sums in electromagnetics and acoustics, but was historically restricted to kernels satisfying a Green's identity, limiting its applicability.

Method: An improved method of proxy points is introduced, allowing precorrected-FFT to handle a wide variety of kernels using only pointwise kernel access (no analytic information). A detailed algorithm for choosing proxy points and spreading grid is provided, implemented in the new pcfft package.

Result: The method handles kernels from non-local PDEs, Matern kernels, anisotropic kernels, and Green's functions of high-order PDEs. It shows favorable scaling compared to other kernel-independent fast summation methods and accelerates integral equation solvers for non-standard kernels.

Conclusion: The improved proxy-point precorrected-FFT broadens applicability to general kernels and demonstrates practical value in surface scattering, geophysics, and microbial community formation applications.

Abstract: The precorrected-FFT has been used to rapidly evaluate $N$-body sums arising in electromagnetics and acoustics settings. It has, however, been historically limited to kernels that satisfy a Green's identity. In this work, we demonstrate that, using an improved version of the method of proxy points, the precorrected-FFT can be used to efficiently compute $N$-body sums with a wide variety of kernels. This algorithm requires no analytic information beyond pointwise access to the kernel. Our examples include kernels from non-local PDEs, Matern kernels, anisotropic kernels, and the Green's functions of high order PDEs. We also provide a detailed algorithm for choosing the proxy points and spreading grid, as implemented in the new pcfft package. We show these algorithmic choices result in favorable scaling when compared to other kernel-independent fast summation methods. Finally, we use the algorithm to accelerate a wide variety of integral equations solvers involving non-standard kernels, with applications in surface scattering, geophysics, and microbial community formation.

</details>


### [5] [Riemannian Hessian analysis of Sobolev gradient flows for the Gross--Pitaevskii ground state problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12833)
*Shixin Zheng,Xiangxiong Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Sobolev gradient flows for the Gross-Pitaevskii ground state problem are analyzed as Riemannian gradient descent on the sphere under discrete elliptic operator-induced metrics, using the condition number of the Riemannian Hessian at the ground state for a uniform convergence analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sobolev gradient flows have been used for the Gross-Pitaevskii ground state problem, but a unified convergence analysis across different metrics was lacking. This work aims to provide a uniform framework to understand and compare such methods, and to inspire new, better-performing metrics.

Method: They analyze convergence via the condition number of the Riemannian Hessian at the ground state. Bounds on the Rayleigh quotient of this Hessian yield a uniform analysis for different metrics, covering classical metrics (shifted Laplacian, energy-adaptive) and three new proposed metrics, including a Thomas-Fermi metric.

Result: The Thomas-Fermi metric has a condition number uniformly bounded in interaction strength for the 1D harmonic trap, and its iteration count does not grow in the Thomas-Fermi regime. Analysis predictions match 3D numerical tests with up to one billion unknowns for both Riemannian gradient descent and conjugate gradient. The Thomas-Fermi flow needs the fewest iterations, while tensor-product-solvable metrics are fastest in time on one GPU.

Conclusion: The uniform Hessian-condition-number analysis explains the performance of Sobolev gradient flows and motivates the Thomas-Fermi metric, which is iteration-efficient and robust to strong interactions, validated by large-scale 3D experiments.

Abstract: Sobolev gradient flows for the Gross--Pitaevskii ground state problem are Riemannian gradient descent methods on the sphere under a metric induced by a discrete elliptic operator. We analyze their convergence through the condition number of the Riemannian Hessian at the ground state. Bounds of the Rayleigh quotient of the Riemannian Hessian give a uniform analysis of these methods for different metrics. The uniform analysis applies to the classical metrics, such as the shifted Laplacian and the energy-adaptive metrics. Inspired by this analysis, we also consider three new metrics. One of them is a Thomas--Fermi metric, whose condition number is bounded uniformly in the interaction strength for the one-dimensional harmonic trap and whose iteration count does not grow in the Thomas--Fermi regime in the numerical tests. The performance suggested by our analysis is consistent with three-dimensional numerical tests with up to one billion unknowns, for both Riemannian gradient descent and Riemannian conjugate gradient methods, whose practical performance is also related to the Hessian condition number. The Thomas--Fermi flow needs the fewest iterations, while the metrics inverted exactly by tensor-product solvers are the fastest in computational time on one GPU card.

</details>


### [6] [Numerical Smoothness and Error Analysis for Evolution Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12888)
*Tong Sun*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Introduces the concept of numerical smoothness for error analysis of evolution equations, yielding a new equivalence theory (Consistency + Numerical Smoothness <=> optimal error estimates) that works for nonlinear problems and schemes, unlike the Lax Equivalence Theorem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Error analysis of nonlinear evolution equations via numerical stability is difficult. The popular error splitting (leading to local error along the original solution and the Lax Equivalence Theorem) does not handle nonlinear problems, nonlinear schemes, and other complications well. The alternative splitting—seen only in a few numerical ODE books—leads to a new concept of numerical smoothness that promises a more effective framework.

Method: Starts from two fundamentally different error splittings. The first defines local error along the original solution with error propagation by numerical schemes; the second defines local error along the numerical solution with error propagation by the solutions of the evolution equations. From the second, the concept of numerical smoothness is developed. Temporal and spatial numerical smoothness indicators are defined, and their necessity for optimal convergence rate is proven, with computable error estimates expressed as functions of these indicators.

Result: Numerical smoothness works naturally in error analysis/estimation/control for nonlinear evolution equations. The boundedness of the temporal and spatial numerical smoothness indicators is proven necessary for optimal convergence rate. Computable error estimates are derived as functions of the smoothness indicators, optimal in both the approximations' convergence rates and the error propagation of the evolution equation.

Conclusion: A new equivalence theory emerges: Consistency + Numerical Smoothness <=> optimal error estimates. Unlike the Lax Equivalence Theorem, this new theory works well for nonlinear problems, nonlinear schemes, and many other complications, offering advantages over the traditional stability-based framework.

Abstract: This is a lecture note to introduce the innovative concept of numerical smoothness and its application in the error analysis of numerical solutions of evolution equations. We start from the two fundamentally different error splittings. One splitting is popular, which brings us to local error defined along the original solution and error propagation by numerical schemes. The well know Lax Equivalence Theorem is based on this error splitting. The other splitting can only be seen in a few numerical ODE books, which brings us to local error defined along the numerical solution and error propagation by the solutions of the evolution equations. The innovative concept of numerical smoothness arises from the second error splitting. While there are many difficulties when we use numerical stability in error analysis of nonlinear evolution equations, we will show that numerical smoothness works naturally in error analysis/estimation/control with advantages in several ways. We will define temporal and spatial numerical smoothness indicators, prove the necessity of the boundedness of these indicators for having optimal convergence rate, and show computable error estimates as functions of these smoothness indicators. Moreover, these error estimates are optimal in the approximatons' convergence rates and also optimal in the error propagation of the evolution equation. In comparison with error analysis framework of the Lax Equivalence Theorem, from the necessity results and the error estimates proven in the numerical smoothness framework, we have a new equivalence theory: Consistency + Numerical Smoothness <=> optimal error estimates. Unlike the Lax Equivalence Theorem, the new equivalence theory works well for nonlinear problems, nonlinear schemes, and many other complications of problems.

</details>


### [7] [Linear and entropy-stable scheme for the dynamic van der Waals model of non-isothermal liquid-vapor flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13029)
*Yunzhuo Guo,Zhonghua Qiao,Xianmin Xu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A linear, decoupled, fully discrete scheme that preserves entropy production for the dynamic van der Waals model describing non-isothermal phase transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic van der Waals models require thermodynamically consistent, entropy-stable numerical schemes, but chain-rule identities fail under discrete differentiation, making such schemes difficult to design.

Method: An equivalent reformulation avoids chain-rule identities; a component-wise convex-splitting method computes discrete chemical potential and temperature, yielding a discrete entropy-stable inequality with no time step restriction. The explicitly treated velocity allows the three conservation laws to be solved linearly and sequentially in a decoupled manner; the momentum equation uses a classical linear semi-implicit scheme.

Result: Numerical experiments verify expected accuracy. Droplet evaporation and 2D/3D Leidenfrost effect simulations capture complex non-isothermal phase-transition dynamics while reproducing discrete entropy production behavior.

Conclusion: The proposed scheme is linear, decoupled, entropy-production-preserving, and time-step-unrestricted, effectively simulating complex non-isothermal phase transitions in single-component liquid-vapor systems.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a linear and decoupled fully discrete scheme that preserves the entropy production property for the dynamic van der Waals model. This model provides a thermodynamically consistent diffuse-interface framework for describing non-isothermal phase transitions in single-component liquid-vapor systems, containing the coupled conservation laws of mass, momentum and energy. We first introduce an equivalent reformulation of the governing equations that avoids the use of chain-rule identities, which generally fail under discrete differentiation. Further, a component-wise convex-splitting method is employed to calculate the discrete chemical potential and temperature. This treatment provides a discrete entropy-stable inequality without a time step restriction. Based on the explicitly treated velocity, the scheme allows the three conservation equations to be solved linearly and sequentially in a decoupled manner. Since the momentum equation does not directly affect the entropy-stability analysis, we use a classical linear semi-implicit scheme to solve it. Numerical experiments verify the expected numerical accuracy. Simulations of droplet evaporation and two- and three-dimensional Leidenfrost effects further demonstrate the proposed method can capture complex non-isothermal phase-transition dynamics while numerically reproducing the discrete entropy production behavior.

</details>


### [8] [Numerical Stability of Extended-Domain Spectral Collocation: Floating-Point Regularization and an Equilibrium Law for the Frame Constants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13222)
*Po-Yi Wu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Extended-domain spectral collocation solves BVPs using the Laplacian eigenbasis of an enclosing box, collocating at as many nodes as modes. Despite exponential ill-conditioning and a theorem ruling out stable geometric convergence for equispaced data, such solvers converge geometrically to a few orders above unit roundoff. The paper explains this in 1D: the error is bounded by nodal residual, distance to a competitor, and two frame constants (leakage and restricted Marcinkiewicz-Zygmund), bounded uniformly in the number of modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extended-domain spectral collocation appears to work well in practice (geometric convergence to a few orders above unit roundoff) despite being exponentially ill-conditioned, with the impossibility theorem for equispaced data seemingly ruling out stable geometric convergence. The authors aim to explain this apparent paradox.

Method: One-dimensional analysis. The collocation solution is the solution operator applied to the interpolant of the data in an operator-dependent test space whose Lebesgue constant must grow exponentially. The floating-point error of any coefficient vector is bounded by its nodal residual, its distance to a competitor, and two frame constants (a leakage constant and a restricted Marcinkiewicz-Zygmund constant), bounded uniformly in the number of modes above a threshold. For a backward-stable solve, under a condition on the perturbed matrix, the bound is uniform in conditioning for analytic data. For the Poisson operator, the tail rate of the two constants is given by a constrained equilibrium problem of logarithmic potential theory.

Result: With computed constants, the error bound is within a factor of about ten of the error floor of a direct solver. For an interval of length L extended by δ at each end, the problems for δ and L²/(4δ) are dual; the rate is linear for δ = L/2 and starts with slope minus the fraction of the extended domain occupied by the extension, so for δ < L/2 the leakage constant obeys no power law with the exponent of the end of the spectrum. In experiments, convection-diffusion operators behave like the Poisson operator at moderate Péclet numbers.

Conclusion: The practical geometric convergence of extended-domain spectral collocation is explained via a floating-point error bound decomposable into nodal residual, distance to a competitor, and two frame constants, whose behavior is characterized by logarithmic potential theory. The analysis clarifies the ill-conditioning paradox and connects leakage/conditioning behavior to domain extension parameters, with convection-diffusion operators resembling the Poisson case at moderate Péclet numbers.

Abstract: Extended-domain spectral collocation expands the solution of a boundary value problem in the Laplacian eigenbasis of an enclosing box and collocates at as many nodes as modes. The square system is exponentially ill-conditioned, and the impossibility theorem for equispaced data rules out stable geometric convergence; yet such solvers converge geometrically to a few orders of magnitude above the unit roundoff. We explain why in one dimension. The collocation solution is the solution operator applied to the interpolant of the data in an operator-dependent test space whose Lebesgue constant must grow exponentially for geometric convergence, so exact solutions can amplify data errors exponentially. In floating-point arithmetic the error of any coefficient vector is bounded by its nodal residual, its distance to a competitor and two frame constants, a leakage and a restricted Marcinkiewicz--Zygmund constant, bounded uniformly in the number of modes at a fixed positive threshold; for a backward-stable solve, under a condition on the perturbed matrix, the bound is uniform in the conditioning for suitably analytic data and, with computed constants, within a factor of about ten of the error floor of a direct solver. For the Poisson operator the rate of the two constants along the tail of the spectrum is given by a constrained equilibrium problem of logarithmic potential theory. For an interval of length $L$ extended by $δ$ at each end, the problems for $δ$ and $L^2/(4δ)$ are dual, the rate is linear for $δ= L/2$, and it starts with slope minus the fraction of the extended domain occupied by the extension, so for $δ< L/2$ the leakage constant obeys no power law with the exponent of the end of the spectrum. In experiments, convection--diffusion operators behave like the Poisson operator at moderate Péclet numbers.

</details>


### [9] [ADER-ZIST - a mixed explicit ADER-DG and zonally implicit space-time DG approach for Cartesian cut cell meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13245)
*Michael Dumbser,Christian Engwer,Sandra May,Peter Munch*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: ADER-ZIST: a conservative high-order mixed explicit-implicit DG scheme for hyperbolic conservation laws on Cartesian cut cell meshes that avoids the small cell problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cut cell meshes on Cartesian grids contain arbitrarily small cells, causing the small cell problem: explicit CFL conditions severely restrict the admissible time step.

Method: Use explicit one-step ADER-DG only on Cartesian cells away from boundaries; discretize the cut cell zone and its face neighbors with an implicit space-time DG scheme coupled via the numerical flux. Curved boundaries use high-order isoparametric sub-triangles; the implicit nonlinear system is solved with an inexact matrix-free Newton-Krylov method. Artificial viscosity with an oscillation-eliminating smoothness sensor handles shocks.

Result: Assessed on compressible Euler test problems (smooth flows, straight/curved boundaries, acoustic scattering, shock-dominated flows). Reaches nominal order N+1 for smooth solutions up to N=5, with minimal cut cell volume fractions down to ~10^-7.

Conclusion: ADER-ZIST is conservative by construction, and its time step is determined only by the Cartesian background cell size, effectively overcoming the small cell problem.

Abstract: We present high order accurate mixed explicit-implicit discontinuous Galerkin (DG) schemes for the solution of hyperbolic systems of conservation laws on Cartesian cut cell meshes. Such meshes may contain arbitrarily small cut cells, which leads to the well-known small cell problem: the CFL condition of explicit schemes would severely restrict the admissible time step. To overcome this difficulty, we use high order explicit fully-discrete one-step ADER-DG schemes only on Cartesian cells away from the embedded boundaries. The zone close to the embedded boundary, which consists of cut cells and their direct face neighbors, is discretized with an implicit space-time DG scheme that is coupled to the explicit scheme directly via the numerical flux. The resulting scheme, called ADER-ZIST (ADER-DG with Zonally Implicit Space-Time DG), is conservative by construction, and its time step is determined only by the size of the Cartesian background cells. Curved embedded boundaries are represented by high order isoparametric sub-triangles inside the cut cells, and the nonlinear system of the implicit cells is solved via an inexact matrix-free Newton-Krylov method. For problems with shock waves, we add an artificial viscosity term whose strength is controlled by an oscillation-eliminating smoothness sensor. ADER-ZIST is assessed on a comprehensive set of test problems for the compressible Euler equations, ranging from smooth flows around straight and curved embedded boundaries to acoustic scattering and shock-dominated flows. The scheme essentially reaches its nominal order of accuracy N+1 for smooth solutions and for polynomial degrees up to N=5, on meshes with minimal volume fractions of the small cut cells down to the order of 10^-7.

</details>


### [10] [On the Convergence of the Mesh-Free Discretization of a Non-Linear Bond-Based Peridynamic Model with a Bond-Breakage Criterion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13355)
*Lukas Pflug,Michael Stingl,Max Zetzmann*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves convergence of a mesh-free discretization scheme for a dynamic non-linear bond-based peridynamic model with irreversible damage, in the natural solution-space norm, and establishes spatial Lipschitz regularity of the continuous solution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mesh-free discretizations of peridynamic models are widely used, but rigorous convergence proofs are scarce, especially for damage models with bond-breakage criteria. The authors aim to justify such schemes for a well-posed regularized operator that models tension-only fracture, while accounting for the practical one-point quadrature employed.

Method: They analyze a mesh-free discretization (with one-point quadrature explicitly included) of a dynamic non-linear bond-based peridynamic model featuring irreversible damage via a bond-breakage criterion, for fixed horizon δ>0. Convergence is shown in C^1([0,T],L^∞(Ω)^d), i.e., in L^∞ spatially. They then derive local Lipschitz bounds for the displacement for locally L-Lipschitz input data, independent of damage.

Result: Convergence of the discretization is established with respect to the natural norm C^1([0,T],L^∞(Ω)^d). The convergence result constrains spatial regularity, and the authors prove local Lipschitz bounds on the displacement for locally L-Lipschitz data, regardless of damage. The δ→0 limit is not treated.

Conclusion: The scheme for the regularized peridynamic fracture model converges in a strong L^∞-based norm, and the continuous solution exhibits local Lipschitz spatial regularity. This supports interpreting the model as an approximation of a truly discontinuous fracture model with length scale δ, though the limit δ→0 is left open.

Abstract: We show the convergence of a commonly used mesh-free discretization of a dynamic non-linear bond-based peridynamic model with irreversible damage implemented via a bond-breakage criterion for a fixed horizon $δ>0$. The model chosen here is a mathematically well-posed regularization of a discontinuous peridynamic operator often found in the literature, which assumes that fracture results from strain in tension only. We explicitly include the one-point quadrature used in this scheme in the analysis. The convergence is with respect to the natural norm of the solution space given by $C^1([0,T],L^\infty(Ω)^d)$, and therefore $L^\infty(Ω)^d$ in the spatial coordinates. This result heavily constrains the spatial regularity of the continuous solution which we prove afterwards. More specifically, for locally $L$-Lipschitz continuous input data, local Lipschitz bounds are derived for the displacement, regardless of damage. This is consistent with interpreting the peridynamic model used here as an approximation to a truly discontinuous fracture model with a length-scale parameter given by the horizon $δ>0$. We note that we do not deal with the limit of the solutions as $δ\searrow 0$.

</details>


### [11] [Global Optimization of Beam Profiles for Suppressing the Bump-On-Tail Instability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13391)
*Sandro Mignelli,Lukas Einkemmer*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks optimization algorithms to find ON/OFF beam profiles that suppress the bump-on-tail instability in plasmas, achieving over 80% instability reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A constant-intensity particle beam propagating into plasma triggers a bump-on-tail instability. Beam modulation can reduce this instability, but finding optimal beam profiles is a complex optimization problem with a rugged landscape and high-dimensional parameter space.

Method: The authors evaluate several optimization algorithms and identify population-based methods (CMA-ES and Differential Evolution) as best performing. They focus on experimentally simpler ON/OFF beam profiles, which yield a higher-dimensional and more challenging optimization problem.

Result: The optimization identifies ON/OFF beam profiles that reduce the instability by more than 80%, significantly outperforming previously reported results in the literature.

Conclusion: Population-based optimization algorithms, particularly CMA-ES and Differential Evolution, are effective for designing beam profiles that strongly suppress the bump-on-tail instability, even under the harder ON/OFF constraint.

Abstract: A particle beam with constant intensity that propagates into a plasma triggers a bump-on-tail instability. It has been demonstrated that beam modulation can be used to significantly reduce this instability. However, finding the optimal beam profiles that accomplish this is a complex optimization problem that suffers from an extremely rugged optimization landscape and a relatively high-dimensional parameter space. In this paper, we evaluate a number of optimization algorithms and find that population based methods (in particular, CMA-ES and Differential Evolution) perform best. In particular, we investigate beam profiles where the beam is either completely turned ON or OFF. This configuration is easier to accomplish experimentally, but leads to a higher-dimensional and thus more challenging optimization problem. We obtain ON/OFF beam profiles that reduce the instability by more than 80% and thus perform significantly better than what has been reported in the literature.

</details>


### [12] [Regularisation and Parameter Choice Rules for Composite Linear Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13483)
*Christina Brandt,Patrick Horn*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A novel regularization strategy for structured linear inverse problems A = F∘G, combining simpler regularizations for F and G with parameter choice rules and convergence analysis, tested on MPI and convolution problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured linear inverse problems of the form Ax = F∘G x = z are challenging to regularize directly, and classical parameter choice rules fail when no source conditions are known because intermediate error bounds may be unavailable.

Method: Proposes a regularization strategy that avoids using operator A or a complex scheme involving both F and G simultaneously. Instead, it combines already existing, simpler regularizations for F and G separately, enabling use of established and optimized programs for high-speed computation. Provides both a-priori and a-posteriori parameter choice rules with convergence speed analysis under source conditions.

Result: The method is tested on the pre-clinical medical imaging technique of magnetic particle imaging (MPI) with different choices of regularization, and on a convolution problem for analysis of convergence speed.

Conclusion: Combining separate regularizations for F and G offers an effective and computationally efficient approach for structured inverse problems, with valid parameter choice rules and convergence guarantees even when source conditions are not classically available.

Abstract: In this paper we propose a novel regularisation strategy for structured linear inverse problems of the form $Ax=F \circ G x = z$. Instead of using the operator $A$ or a complex scheme involving both $F$ and $G$ simultaneously, combining already existing and simpler regularisations for $F$ and $G$ allows the use of established and optimised programs for high-speed computations. As classical parameter choice rules fail in the scenario when no source conditions are known because intermediate error bounds may be unavailable, we provide both a-priori and a-posteriori rules with a convergence speed analysis given source conditions. We test the proposed method on the pre-clinical medical imaging technique of magnetic particle imaging with different choices of regularisation and on a convolution problem for an analysis of the convergence speed.

</details>


### [13] [On a maximum principle in discretization with spherical harmonics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13540)
*Arnold Reusken*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper derives sharp radius estimates guaranteeing the maximum principle for the harmonic extension of boundary data approximated by spherical harmonics of degree at most N on the d-dimensional unit sphere.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spherical harmonics are widely used, and the Laplace equation on the unit sphere with spherical harmonic boundary data is a classical application. While the continuous solution obeys the maximum principle, the discrete/approximated solution u_N generally does not. This raises the question of how far the maximum principle extends radially.

Method: The authors consider general dimension d >= 2, approximate boundary data in the finite-dimensional space spanned by all spherical harmonics of degree at most N, and analyze the resulting discrete harmonic solution u_N. They derive sharp estimates on the largest radius r_N^max such that the maximum principle holds for all x with ||x|| <= r_N^max, and study the sharpness of these estimates.

Result: For d = 3 they show r_N^max >~ 1 - 3 N^{-1} ln(2^{1/3} N), with similar estimates derived for d != 3.

Conclusion: The maximum principle for the spherical-harmonic approximation of the Laplace equation holds within a radial region approaching the unit sphere as N grows, with explicit dimension-dependent bounds, and these bounds are shown to be sharp.

Abstract: Spherical harmonics have been studied extensively and are used in many fields of application. We consider the probably earliest application of spherical harmonics, namely for a representation of the solution of the Laplace equation on the $d$-dimensional unit sphere where the boundary data are represented in a spherical harmonics basis. We consider general $d \geq 2$. For the solution $u$ of the Laplace equation the fundamental maximum principle holds. If one approximates the boundary data in the finite dimensional space spanned by all spherical harmonics of degree at most $N$, the resulting discrete solution, denoted by $u_N$, does in general not satisfy the maximum principle. In this paper we derive sharp estimates for $r_N^{\rm max}$ such that the maximum principle holds for $u_N(x)$ for all $x \in \Bbb{R}^d$ with $\|x\| \leq r_N^{\rm max}$. We show that for $d=3$ one has $r_N^{\rm max} \gtrsim 1- 3 N^{-1} \ln\big( 2^\frac13 N \big) $. Similar estimates are derived for $d \neq 3$. We also study the sharpness of these estimates.

</details>


### [14] [Preconditioning proximal Galerkin: mesh and degree robust solvers for variational inequalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13591)
*Ioannis P. A. Papadopoulos,Pablo D. Brubeck*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A robust block preconditioner enables scalable iterative solution of proximal Galerkin variational inequalities, using a primal Schur complement approach that makes large 3D simulations (10M+ DOF) feasible.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Proximal Galerkin is a structure-preserving solver for variational inequalities with discretization-independent nonlinear iteration counts, but in large 3D simulations the arising Newton saddle point systems are infeasible to solve with direct methods. An iterative linear solver is needed, which requires a robust, scalable preconditioner.

Method: The authors design a block preconditioner for the primal Schur complement (rather than the usual dual Schur complement acting on the latent variable). It requires two approximate inverses of the latent block (an algebraic equation cheaply inverted after small perturbation) and only one of the primal Schur complement. Two approximations for the primal Schur complement are introduced: a PDE-operator-based approach and an algebraic approach, both efficiently invertible by geometric multigrid.

Result: Tested on obstacle, Signorini, and strain-constrained elasticity problems, and compared against monolithic multigrid. Standard relaxation methods yield robust iteration counts with respect to mesh size and discretization degree. For Signorini, specialized relaxation and prolongation handle nearly-incompressible materials. Linear solves are parameter-robust in 2D with mild growth in 3D, enabling solution of a 3D contact problem with Poisson's ratio 0.499 and over ten million degrees of freedom.

Conclusion: The proposed primal-Schur-complement block preconditioner makes proximal Galerkin scalable for large 3D variational inequality problems, including nearly-incompressible contact, while maintaining robust iteration counts across mesh sizes and discretization degrees.

Abstract: The proximal Galerkin algorithm is a structure-preserving solver for variational inequalities that results in discretization-independent nonlinear iteration counts. However, in large three-dimensional simulations, the arising Newton saddle point systems cannot be feasibly solved by direct methods. To leverage an iterative linear solver, we propose a robust and scalable block preconditioner. Instead of using the usual dual Schur complement that acts on the latent variable, we design a preconditioner for the primal Schur complement. This leads to a block preconditioner that requires two approximate inverses of the latent block and only one of the primal Schur complement. The latent block represents an algebraic equation which, after a small perturbation, can be cheaply inverted. We then introduce two choices of approximations for the primal Schur complement: a PDE-operator-based and an algebraic approach. Either approximation can be efficiently inverted by geometric multigrid.
  We investigate the effectiveness of these strategies when proximal Galerkin is applied to obstacle, Signorini, and strain-constrained elasticity problems and also compare with a monolithic multigrid approach. Choices of standard relaxation methods, in the block preconditioner route, lead to robust iteration counts with respect to the mesh size and discretization degree. Moreover, in the context of the Signorini, specialized relaxation and prolongation also result in effective solvers for nearly-incompressible elastic materials. The linear solves are parameter-robust in 2D, with only mild growth in 3D. This allows for the solution of a three-dimensional contact problem, with a Poisson's ratio of 0.499, discretized with over ten million degrees of freedom.

</details>


### [15] [Energy conserving compatible space--time FEM for deterministic and stochastic rotating shallow water](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13624)
*Maneesh Kumar Singh,Colin J. Cotter*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A fully discrete, arbitrary-order space-time finite element scheme for rotating shallow water equations achieves exact energy conservation, extended to stochastic forcing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Energy conservation in numerical discretisations of the rotating shallow water equations is difficult to achieve exactly, especially with high-order accuracy in both space and time.

Method: Space-time finite element discretisation using compatible finite element methods in space and continuous Petrov-Galerkin schemes in time, with projected auxiliary variables for Hamiltonian derivatives. An iterative solver based on monolithic patch smoothers drives a Newton method to convergence. Extended to the energy-conserving Stochastic Forcing by Lie Transport formulation.

Result: Numerical tests demonstrate convergence, conservation, and robustness of the scheme, with solver-induced energy errors kept very small.

Conclusion: The scheme provides a fully discrete, arbitrarily high-order, energy-conserving discretisation of rotating shallow water equations, robustly solvable and extendable to stochastic transport formulations.

Abstract: We present a fully discrete energy conserving discretisation of the rotating shallow water equations with arbitrary order in space and time. A space time finite element discretisation is used, built from compatible finite element methods in space and continuous Petrov-Galerkin schemes in time. Projected auxiliary variables for Hamiltonian derivatives are necessary for exact energy conservation. We provide an iterative solver approach based on monolithic patch smoothers, which allows us to efficiently converge a Newton method sufficiently that energy errors due to the solver are very small. We extend this construction to the energy conserving Stochastic Forcing by Lie Transport formulation. Then we demonstrate the convergence, conservation and robustness in numerical tests.

</details>


### [16] [Analysis of an inexact Schwarz domain decomposition method in 3D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13793)
*Nibedita Ghosh*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Analysis of an inexact 3D Schwarz domain decomposition method for a Laplace problem on two overlapping spheres, where subproblems are solved approximately via L2-orthogonal projection onto spherical harmonics of degree ≤N. A new maximum principle variant is developed to prove convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The model problem arises from the ddCOSMO solver for implicit solvation in computational chemistry, requiring an efficient and provably convergent method for solving Laplace problems on overlapping spherical domains.

Method: An inexact Schwarz domain decomposition method on a domain formed by two overlapping spheres. Each subproblem is solved approximately by L2-orthogonally projecting its boundary data onto the space of spherical harmonics of degree at most N. The analysis introduces a new variant of the maximum principle.

Result: A new variant of the maximum principle is established, which enables the analysis of the Schwarz-spherical domain decomposition method.

Conclusion: The inexact Schwarz method with spherical harmonic projection is analytically tractable, and the new maximum principle provides the theoretical foundation for its convergence analysis.

Abstract: We consider an inexact version of the Schwarz domain decomposition method in $3$D to solve a Laplace problem in a domain that is the union of two overlapping spheres. We solve each subproblem approximately by projecting its boundary data $L^2$-orthogonally onto the space of spherical harmonics of degree at most $N$. The model problem arises from the ddCOSMO solver used for implicit solvation in computational chemistry. We analyze the Schwarz-spherical domain decomposition method by establishing a new variant of the maximum principle.

</details>


### [17] [Fast and Stable Arbitrary Precision Approximate Matrix Multiplication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13905)
*Ioana Dumitriu,Olga Holtz,Abraham Hsu,Nicholas Liskij,Yoav Moran,Ryan Schneider,Oded Schwartz,Shuncheng Yuan*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper advances the theory and practice of Arbitrary Precision Approximate (APA) matrix multiplication, presenting a new search algorithm, an addition-minimizing optimization using alternative bases, and a stability proof for a Bini-style exact algorithm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: APA algorithms can have lower asymptotic cost than many exact fast matrix multiplication schemes but remain understudied, with little known about their numerical properties, despite existing for nearly half a century.

Method: The authors develop a new, easy-to-use search algorithm for APA routines that automatically generates encoding matrices with functional entries. They combine this insight with alternative bases to minimize the number of additions, and analyze the stability of Bini's algorithm that computes exact products via a modified APA scheme.

Result: The search algorithm matches or beats the best known APA algorithms for several sizes and improves the upper bound on the border rank of <2,4,5> matrix multiplication. Using alternative bases reduces the number of additions and improves the leading coefficient. The stability of fast exact matrix multiplication is proven for Bini's algorithm with or without alternative bases.

Conclusion: APA matrix multiplication is practically viable and theoretically rich: improved algorithms and border rank bounds are achievable, leading coefficients can be optimized, and Bini-style APA-based exact multiplication enjoys the same stability as fast exact methods.

Abstract: We present several new and surprising results on the theory and practice of Arbitrary Precision Approximate (APA) matrix multiplication, which refers to algorithms that output merely approximations to matrix multiplication even in exact arithmetic. While APA algorithms have been around for nearly half a century, and despite the fact that they have potentially lower (asymptotic) cost than many fast exact matrix multiplication schemes, they have not been studied as closely, to the extent that relatively little is known about their numerical properties. In this paper, we shine a spotlight on these algorithms by presenting three important results. First, we provide a new search algorithm for APA routines that is easy to use, automatically generates encoding matrices with functional entries, and can match or beat the best known APA algorithms for several sizes. In particular, we improve the upper bound on the border rank of $\langle 2,4,5 \rangle$ matrix multiplication. Second, we combine this insight with alternative bases to minimize the number of additions in an APA algorithm, thereby improving its leading coefficient. Finally, we prove the same kind of stability that characterizes fast exact matrix multiplication for an algorithm of Bini [Calcolo 1980] that computes an exact product of two matrices using a modified APA scheme (with or without alternative bases).

</details>


### [18] [Fourier Transform-Based Computation of Two-Electron Four-Center Coulomb Repulsion Integrals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12608)
*William Chen,Lior Silberman,Ury Segal,Chen Greif*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A Fourier-based method computes two-electron four-center Coulomb repulsion integrals over Slater-type orbitals by precomputing spherical Fourier-Bessel coefficients, reducing the problem to 1D radial quadrature, achieving ~1e-9 accuracy and ~4.2e5 integrals/s on an NVIDIA V100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-electron four-center Coulomb repulsion integrals over Slater-type orbitals are costly to evaluate, motivating a faster, accurate approach.

Method: Fourier-based method using precomputed and reused spherical Fourier-Bessel coefficients of convolution factors; the spherical part is evaluated directly, leaving only a one-dimensional radial quadrature.

Result: For ten reference integrals, the largest absolute discrepancy is 1.7e-9. A preliminary resident-cache benchmark computes about 4.2e5 unique integrals per second on an NVIDIA V100 at the contraction stage.

Conclusion: The method provides high accuracy and promising GPU performance, with precomputation reuse and reduced quadrature dimensionality.

Abstract: We present a Fourier-based method for evaluating two-electron four-center Coulomb repulsion integrals over Slater-type orbitals. Spherical Fourier-Bessel coefficients of the convolution factors are precomputed and reused. Since the spherical part of the integral can be evaluated directly, there remains only a one-dimensional quadrature problem in the radial direction. For the ten reference integrals reported here, the largest absolute discrepancy is $1.7\times10^{-9}$. A preliminary resident-cache benchmark computes approximately $4.2\times10^5$ unique integrals per second on an NVIDIA V100 at the contraction stage.

</details>


### [19] [Median randomized quasi-Monte Carlo for nested integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12643)
*Arved Bartuska,Yang Liu,Raúl Tempone*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper extends median randomized quasi-Monte Carlo (mRQMC) to nested integrals, proposing a double-loop mRQMC estimator with super-polynomial convergence, plus an importance-sampling regularization scheme for EIG applications where outer integrand regularity is lacking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nested integrals arise in Bayesian experimental design, medical decision making, and computational finance. Existing nested-integral estimators (double-loop or multilevel Monte Carlo) only achieve algebraic convergence, while mRQMC has shown super-polynomial rates for standard integrals—so extending mRQMC to the nested setting is desirable.

Method: A double-loop mRQMC estimator is proposed, achieving super-polynomial convergence when both inner and outer integrands are analytic. For expected information gain (EIG), where the outer integrand lacks regularity, a theoretically-grounded importance sampling regularization scheme is introduced.

Result: Super-polynomial convergence is achieved for the inner integral; for the outer integral in EIG, only an algebraic rate α+3/2−ε (ε>0) is guaranteed, where α is the order of mixed derivatives of the regularized outer integrand. Substantial improvements over double-loop RQMC with sample mean are shown in moderately high dimensions, and improved convergence for EIG estimation is demonstrated.

Conclusion: Double-loop mRQMC with importance sampling regularization outperforms established nested-integral estimators, offering a practical and theoretically supported approach, especially for EIG estimation.

Abstract: Nested integrals $\int f\left(\int g(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}{}\boldsymbol{x}\right)\mathrm{d}{}\boldsymbol{y}$ where $\boldsymbol{y}\in[0,1]^{d_1}$ and $\boldsymbol{x}\in[0,1]^{d_2}$ frequently arise in Bayesian experimental design, medical decision making, and computational finance. Statistical estimators for nested integrals are more computationally demanding than for standard integrals. Recent studies demonstrate that the median randomized quasi-Monte Carlo (mRQMC) method achieves super-polynomial convergence rates for analytic integrands. We extend mRQMC methods to nested integrations and propose a novel double-loop mRQMC estimator with super-polynomial convergence rate if both the inner and outer integrands are analytic. Established estimators for nested integrals, such as double-loop or multilevel Monte Carlo, are by contrast limited to algebraic convergence rates. However, expected information gain (EIG) applications from experimental design typically do not fulfill this condition due to a lack of regularity in the outer integrand. We address this through a theoretically-grounded importance sampling regularization scheme. Although super-polynomial convergence rates remain achievable for the inner integral, only the algebraic rate $α+3/2-ε$ for any $ε>0$ can be theoretically guaranteed for the outer integral in the EIG, where $α\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}$ denotes the order of the mixed derivatives of the regularized outer integrand. Our approach shows substantial improvements over double-loop randomized quasi-Monte Carlo methods based on the sample mean in moderately high-dimensional settings for a simple polynomial example. Moreover, we demonstrate improved convergence behavior of the median-based estimator for the more challenging setting of EIG estimation.

</details>


### [20] [Variational neural transport for aggregation-diffusion equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12745)
*Qianyi Li,Hailiang Liu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes a neural transport framework for nonlinear, nonlocal aggregation-diffusion equations, reformulating them as a constrained optimal control problem via the Onsager variational principle, with a neural-network-parameterized velocity field and rigorous error analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aggregation-diffusion equations arise in chemotaxis, polymer dynamics, and related fields, but they are nonlinear and nonlocal, making them challenging to solve. There is a need for a flexible, accurate computational framework with theoretical guarantees.

Method: Based on the Onsager variational principle, the governing equation is reformulated as a constrained optimal control problem with the velocity field as control and density evolving via the continuity equation. The velocity field is parameterized by a neural network trained on the variational objective along particle trajectories. Density and generalized score equations are coupled into a single system solved via the Euler method, enabling efficient score propagation for loss evaluation. A rigorous error analysis decomposes total error into approximation, optimization, Monte Carlo sampling, and time-discretization components.

Result: Rigorous error bounds are proven for each decomposed error component. Numerical experiments on several representative models confirm the accuracy and effectiveness of the approach.

Conclusion: The neural transport framework provides an effective and theoretically grounded method for solving nonlinear, nonlocal aggregation-diffusion equations.

Abstract: We develop a neural transport framework for nonlinear, nonlocal aggregationdiffusion equations arising in chemotaxis, polymer dynamics, and related applications. Based on the Onsager variational principle, we reformulate the governing equation as a constrained optimal control problem, taking the velocity field as the control and evolving the density via the associated continuity equation. The velocity field is parameterized by a neural network trained on the variational objective evaluated along particle trajectories. By coupling the density and generalized score equations into a single system solved via the Euler method, the score function propagates efficiently for loss evaluation. Furthermore, we establish a rigorous error analysis that decomposes the total error into approximation, optimization, Monte Carlo sampling, and time-discretization components, and prove corresponding error bounds. Numerical experiments across several representative models demonstrate the accuracy and effectiveness of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [21] [A kernel-independent precorrected-FFT method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12821)
*Tristan Goodwill,Owen Melia*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper extends precorrected-FFT to handle arbitrary kernels without requiring Green's identity, using an improved method of proxy points, enabling fast N-body summation for a wide range of kernels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precorrected-FFT was historically limited to kernels satisfying a Green's identity, restricting its use to standard electromagnetics and acoustics settings. Many kernels of interest (non-local PDEs, Matern, anisotropic, high-order PDE Green's functions) could not benefit from it.

Method: Use an improved method of proxy points to allow precorrected-FFT to compute N-body sums for a wide variety of kernels, requiring only pointwise kernel access (no analytic info). Provide a detailed algorithm for choosing proxy points and spreading grid, implemented in the pcfft package.

Result: The algorithm achieves favorable scaling compared to other kernel-independent fast summation methods, and accelerates a variety of integral equation solvers involving non-standard kernels.

Conclusion: The improved proxy-point precorrected-FFT is a kernel-independent, broadly applicable fast summation method, demonstrated on applications in surface scattering, geophysics, and microbial community formation.

Abstract: The precorrected-FFT has been used to rapidly evaluate $N$-body sums arising in electromagnetics and acoustics settings. It has, however, been historically limited to kernels that satisfy a Green's identity. In this work, we demonstrate that, using an improved version of the method of proxy points, the precorrected-FFT can be used to efficiently compute $N$-body sums with a wide variety of kernels. This algorithm requires no analytic information beyond pointwise access to the kernel. Our examples include kernels from non-local PDEs, Matern kernels, anisotropic kernels, and the Green's functions of high order PDEs. We also provide a detailed algorithm for choosing the proxy points and spreading grid, as implemented in the new pcfft package. We show these algorithmic choices result in favorable scaling when compared to other kernel-independent fast summation methods. Finally, we use the algorithm to accelerate a wide variety of integral equations solvers involving non-standard kernels, with applications in surface scattering, geophysics, and microbial community formation.

</details>


### [22] [Riemannian Hessian analysis of Sobolev gradient flows for the Gross--Pitaevskii ground state problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12833)
*Shixin Zheng,Xiangxiong Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes Sobolev gradient flows for the Gross-Pitaevskii ground state as Riemannian gradient descent on the sphere, using the Hessian condition number for convergence analysis, and proposes new metrics including a Thomas-Fermi metric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sobolev gradient flows for the GP ground state problem lack a unified convergence analysis across different metrics, and it is unclear how metric choice affects iteration count and computational efficiency.

Method: Model the flows as Riemannian gradient descent on the sphere under a discrete elliptic operator-induced metric. Analyze convergence via the condition number of the Riemannian Hessian at the ground state, using Rayleigh quotient bounds of the Hessian for a uniform analysis across metrics.

Result: The uniform analysis covers classical metrics (shifted Laplacian, energy-adaptive). Three new metrics are proposed; the Thomas-Fermi metric has condition number bounded uniformly in interaction strength for the 1D harmonic trap and non-growing iteration count in the TF regime. Consistent with 3D tests up to one billion unknowns.

Conclusion: The Hessian condition number predicts practical performance of Riemannian gradient descent and conjugate gradient. The Thomas-Fermi flow needs the fewest iterations, while exactly-invertible tensor-product metrics are fastest on a single GPU card.

Abstract: Sobolev gradient flows for the Gross--Pitaevskii ground state problem are Riemannian gradient descent methods on the sphere under a metric induced by a discrete elliptic operator. We analyze their convergence through the condition number of the Riemannian Hessian at the ground state. Bounds of the Rayleigh quotient of the Riemannian Hessian give a uniform analysis of these methods for different metrics. The uniform analysis applies to the classical metrics, such as the shifted Laplacian and the energy-adaptive metrics. Inspired by this analysis, we also consider three new metrics. One of them is a Thomas--Fermi metric, whose condition number is bounded uniformly in the interaction strength for the one-dimensional harmonic trap and whose iteration count does not grow in the Thomas--Fermi regime in the numerical tests. The performance suggested by our analysis is consistent with three-dimensional numerical tests with up to one billion unknowns, for both Riemannian gradient descent and Riemannian conjugate gradient methods, whose practical performance is also related to the Hessian condition number. The Thomas--Fermi flow needs the fewest iterations, while the metrics inverted exactly by tensor-product solvers are the fastest in computational time on one GPU card.

</details>


### [23] [Numerical Smoothness and Error Analysis for Evolution Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12888)
*Tong Sun*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A lecture note introducing "numerical smoothness" for error analysis of evolution equations, offering a new equivalence theory: Consistency + Numerical Smoothness <=> optimal error estimates, valid for nonlinear problems and schemes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The classical Lax Equivalence Theorem relies on error splitting along the original solution and numerical stability, which faces many difficulties in error analysis of nonlinear evolution equations. A new framework is needed that naturally handles nonlinear problems and schemes.

Method: Starts from two fundamentally different error splittings. The second splitting (local error along the numerical solution, error propagation by the evolution equation solutions) gives rise to numerical smoothness. Temporal and spatial numerical smoothness indicators are defined, boundedness necessity proven, and computable error estimates derived as functions of these indicators.

Result: Numerical smoothness indicators are defined; their boundedness is proven necessary for optimal convergence rate; computable error estimates are obtained that are optimal in convergence rate and error propagation.

Conclusion: A new equivalence theory emerges: Consistency + Numerical Smoothness <=> optimal error estimates. Unlike the Lax Equivalence Theorem, it works well for nonlinear problems, nonlinear schemes, and other complications.

Abstract: This is a lecture note to introduce the innovative concept of numerical smoothness and its application in the error analysis of numerical solutions of evolution equations. We start from the two fundamentally different error splittings. One splitting is popular, which brings us to local error defined along the original solution and error propagation by numerical schemes. The well know Lax Equivalence Theorem is based on this error splitting. The other splitting can only be seen in a few numerical ODE books, which brings us to local error defined along the numerical solution and error propagation by the solutions of the evolution equations. The innovative concept of numerical smoothness arises from the second error splitting. While there are many difficulties when we use numerical stability in error analysis of nonlinear evolution equations, we will show that numerical smoothness works naturally in error analysis/estimation/control with advantages in several ways. We will define temporal and spatial numerical smoothness indicators, prove the necessity of the boundedness of these indicators for having optimal convergence rate, and show computable error estimates as functions of these smoothness indicators. Moreover, these error estimates are optimal in the approximatons' convergence rates and also optimal in the error propagation of the evolution equation. In comparison with error analysis framework of the Lax Equivalence Theorem, from the necessity results and the error estimates proven in the numerical smoothness framework, we have a new equivalence theory: Consistency + Numerical Smoothness <=> optimal error estimates. Unlike the Lax Equivalence Theorem, the new equivalence theory works well for nonlinear problems, nonlinear schemes, and many other complications of problems.

</details>


### [24] [Linear and entropy-stable scheme for the dynamic van der Waals model of non-isothermal liquid-vapor flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13029)
*Yunzhuo Guo,Zhonghua Qiao,Xianmin Xu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A linear, decoupled fully discrete scheme is proposed for the dynamic van der Waals model that preserves the entropy production property without time step restrictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The dynamic van der Waals model describes non-isothermal phase transitions via coupled mass, momentum, and energy conservation laws. A key challenge is designing a discretization that preserves the entropy production property while remaining computationally efficient (linear and decoupled).

Method: The authors introduce an equivalent reformulation avoiding chain-rule identities that fail under discrete differentiation. A component-wise convex-splitting method computes discrete chemical potential and temperature, yielding a discrete entropy-stable inequality without time step restriction. With velocity treated explicitly, the three conservation equations are solved linearly and sequentially in a decoupled manner; the momentum equation uses a classical linear semi-implicit scheme since it does not affect entropy stability.

Result: Numerical experiments confirm the expected accuracy. Simulations of droplet evaporation and 2D/3D Leidenfrost effects capture complex non-isothermal phase-transition dynamics while reproducing the discrete entropy production behavior.

Conclusion: The proposed scheme is entropy-stable, linear, and decoupled, making it an effective and efficient tool for simulating complex non-isothermal phase-transition dynamics in the dynamic van der Waals model.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a linear and decoupled fully discrete scheme that preserves the entropy production property for the dynamic van der Waals model. This model provides a thermodynamically consistent diffuse-interface framework for describing non-isothermal phase transitions in single-component liquid-vapor systems, containing the coupled conservation laws of mass, momentum and energy. We first introduce an equivalent reformulation of the governing equations that avoids the use of chain-rule identities, which generally fail under discrete differentiation. Further, a component-wise convex-splitting method is employed to calculate the discrete chemical potential and temperature. This treatment provides a discrete entropy-stable inequality without a time step restriction. Based on the explicitly treated velocity, the scheme allows the three conservation equations to be solved linearly and sequentially in a decoupled manner. Since the momentum equation does not directly affect the entropy-stability analysis, we use a classical linear semi-implicit scheme to solve it. Numerical experiments verify the expected numerical accuracy. Simulations of droplet evaporation and two- and three-dimensional Leidenfrost effects further demonstrate the proposed method can capture complex non-isothermal phase-transition dynamics while numerically reproducing the discrete entropy production behavior.

</details>


### [25] [Numerical Stability of Extended-Domain Spectral Collocation: Floating-Point Regularization and an Equilibrium Law for the Frame Constants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13222)
*Po-Yi Wu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper explains, in one dimension, why extended-domain spectral collocation converges geometrically to a few orders above unit roundoff despite being exponentially ill-conditioned and violating the impossibility theorem for equispaced data, using a floating-point error bound with frame (leakage and restricted Marcinkiewicz–Zygmund) constants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extended-domain spectral collocation is exponentially ill-conditioned and the impossibility theorem for equispaced data rules out stable geometric convergence, yet such solvers still converge geometrically to a few orders above unit roundoff. The paper aims to explain this apparent contradiction.

Method: The collocation solution is viewed as the solution operator applied to the interpolant of the data in an operator-dependent test space whose Lebesgue constant grows exponentially. In floating-point arithmetic, the error of any coefficient vector is bounded by its nodal residual, distance to a competitor, and two frame constants: a leakage constant and a restricted Marcinkiewicz–Zygmund constant, bounded uniformly in the number of modes. A constrained equilibrium problem of logarithmic potential theory gives the tail rate of these constants for the Poisson operator.

Result: For a backward-stable solve under a perturbed-matrix condition, the bound is uniform in the conditioning for suitably analytic data and, with computed constants, lies within about a factor of ten of a direct solver's error floor. For an interval of length L extended by δ, the problems for δ and L²/(4δ) are dual; the rate is linear at δ = L/2 and begins with slope minus the fraction of the extended domain occupied by the extension, so for δ < L/2 the leakage constant obeys no power law with the exponent of the spectrum's end. Convection–diffusion operators behave like the Poisson operator at moderate Péclet numbers.

Conclusion: The geometric convergence of extended-domain spectral collocation is reconciled with its ill-conditioning via a floating-point error analysis and potential-theoretic rates, showing the error floor is governed by frame constants that stay modestly bounded.

Abstract: Extended-domain spectral collocation expands the solution of a boundary value problem in the Laplacian eigenbasis of an enclosing box and collocates at as many nodes as modes. The square system is exponentially ill-conditioned, and the impossibility theorem for equispaced data rules out stable geometric convergence; yet such solvers converge geometrically to a few orders of magnitude above the unit roundoff. We explain why in one dimension. The collocation solution is the solution operator applied to the interpolant of the data in an operator-dependent test space whose Lebesgue constant must grow exponentially for geometric convergence, so exact solutions can amplify data errors exponentially. In floating-point arithmetic the error of any coefficient vector is bounded by its nodal residual, its distance to a competitor and two frame constants, a leakage and a restricted Marcinkiewicz--Zygmund constant, bounded uniformly in the number of modes at a fixed positive threshold; for a backward-stable solve, under a condition on the perturbed matrix, the bound is uniform in the conditioning for suitably analytic data and, with computed constants, within a factor of about ten of the error floor of a direct solver. For the Poisson operator the rate of the two constants along the tail of the spectrum is given by a constrained equilibrium problem of logarithmic potential theory. For an interval of length $L$ extended by $δ$ at each end, the problems for $δ$ and $L^2/(4δ)$ are dual, the rate is linear for $δ= L/2$, and it starts with slope minus the fraction of the extended domain occupied by the extension, so for $δ< L/2$ the leakage constant obeys no power law with the exponent of the end of the spectrum. In experiments, convection--diffusion operators behave like the Poisson operator at moderate Péclet numbers.

</details>


### [26] [ADER-ZIST - a mixed explicit ADER-DG and zonally implicit space-time DG approach for Cartesian cut cell meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13245)
*Michael Dumbser,Christian Engwer,Sandra May,Peter Munch*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces ADER-ZIST, a high-order mixed explicit-implicit discontinuous Galerkin scheme for hyperbolic conservation laws on Cartesian cut cell meshes, solving the small cell problem while maintaining high accuracy and conservation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cartesian cut cell meshes can contain arbitrarily small cut cells, causing the small cell problem: explicit schemes' CFL condition severely restricts the admissible time step.

Method: Use explicit one-step ADER-DG only on Cartesian cells away from boundaries; discretize the cut-cell zone (cut cells plus face neighbors) with an implicit space-time DG scheme coupled to the explicit scheme via numerical flux. Curved boundaries use high-order isoparametric sub-triangles; the implicit nonlinear system is solved via a matrix-free Newton-Krylov method; artificial viscosity with an oscillation-eliminating smoothness sensor handles shocks.

Result: On compressible Euler tests (smooth flows, curved boundaries, acoustic scattering, shock-dominated flows), the scheme essentially reaches nominal order N+1 for smooth solutions up to N=5, on meshes with minimal cut-cell volume fractions down to ~10^-7.

Conclusion: ADER-ZIST is conservative by construction, its time step depends only on the Cartesian background cell size, and it achieves high-order accuracy even with extremely small cut cells.

Abstract: We present high order accurate mixed explicit-implicit discontinuous Galerkin (DG) schemes for the solution of hyperbolic systems of conservation laws on Cartesian cut cell meshes. Such meshes may contain arbitrarily small cut cells, which leads to the well-known small cell problem: the CFL condition of explicit schemes would severely restrict the admissible time step. To overcome this difficulty, we use high order explicit fully-discrete one-step ADER-DG schemes only on Cartesian cells away from the embedded boundaries. The zone close to the embedded boundary, which consists of cut cells and their direct face neighbors, is discretized with an implicit space-time DG scheme that is coupled to the explicit scheme directly via the numerical flux. The resulting scheme, called ADER-ZIST (ADER-DG with Zonally Implicit Space-Time DG), is conservative by construction, and its time step is determined only by the size of the Cartesian background cells. Curved embedded boundaries are represented by high order isoparametric sub-triangles inside the cut cells, and the nonlinear system of the implicit cells is solved via an inexact matrix-free Newton-Krylov method. For problems with shock waves, we add an artificial viscosity term whose strength is controlled by an oscillation-eliminating smoothness sensor. ADER-ZIST is assessed on a comprehensive set of test problems for the compressible Euler equations, ranging from smooth flows around straight and curved embedded boundaries to acoustic scattering and shock-dominated flows. The scheme essentially reaches its nominal order of accuracy N+1 for smooth solutions and for polynomial degrees up to N=5, on meshes with minimal volume fractions of the small cut cells down to the order of 10^-7.

</details>


### [27] [On the Convergence of the Mesh-Free Discretization of a Non-Linear Bond-Based Peridynamic Model with a Bond-Breakage Criterion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13355)
*Lukas Pflug,Michael Stingl,Max Zetzmann*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves convergence of a mesh-free discretization (with one-point quadrature) of a dynamic non-linear bond-based peridynamic model with irreversible damage via a bond-breakage criterion, for fixed horizon δ>0, in the C^1([0,T],L^∞(Ω)^d) norm, and derives local Lipschitz bounds on the displacement for locally L-Lipschitz input data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A commonly used mesh-free discretization of a dynamic non-linear bond-based peridynamic model with irreversible damage needs rigorous convergence analysis.
