### [115] [Parallel Design of a GPU-Resident Semi-Lagrangian Drift-Kinetic Solver on Unstructured Extruded Tokamak Meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12585)
*Mark F. Adams*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A parallel/GPU design for a 3D+2V semi-Lagrangian discontinuous Galerkin drift-kinetic tokamak solver on unstructured flux-surface-aligned grids, using velocity-dependent ragged halos and a toroidal-angle/poloidal-plane/magnetic-moment decomposition that scales to 256 GPUs with 57-75% weak-scaling efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SL schemes are unconditionally stable and DG integration conserves mass along field lines, making them attractive for drift-kinetic tokamak simulation. However, the growing, velocity-dependent halo depth from magnetic-field-line transport and the 5D phase space make efficient parallel and GPU scaling challenging.

Method: A 3X+2V SLDG drift-kinetic solver on unstructured, flux-surface-aligned poloidal planes extruded toroidally. Transport follows field lines across planes; each velocity channel advances a speed-proportional distance, so halo depth grows with speed. Velocity-dependent ragged halos send each channel only its upwind depth. The 4D decomposition spans toroidal angle, 2D poloidal plane, and magnetic moment, keeping parallel velocity whole (no stage transports across magnetic moment). Implemented with an AI code assistant under a machine-checked regression discipline classifying changes into four exactness classes.

Result: On NVIDIA A100s, strong-scales over the magnetic-moment axis from 1 to 12 four-GPU nodes. A weak series of three points refining all five phase-space dimensions together (16x per point) reaches 75% weak-scaling efficiency on 16 GPUs and 57% on 256, from a one-GPU base. Full-step mass drift stays below 2.1x10^-5 at every series point despite non-conservative pointwise poloidal-plane interpolations.

Conclusion: The velocity-dependent ragged-halo and magnetic-moment-preserving decomposition enable efficient GPU parallelization of the SLDG tokamak solver up to 256 GPUs, with well-controlled mass drift, demonstrating a viable path for large-scale drift-kinetic simulation.

Abstract: Semi-Lagrangian (SL) schemes advect a distribution function along characteristics and remap it to a fixed grid. They are unconditionally stable without a global solve, and with discontinuous Galerkin (DG) integration the transfer along a magnetic field line conserves mass exactly. This paper presents the parallel and GPU design of a 3X+2V SLDG drift-kinetic tokamak solver on unstructured, flux-surface-aligned poloidal planes of shaped cross section, extruded toroidally. Transport follows magnetic field lines across the planes, and each velocity channel advances a fixed distance per step, proportional to its parallel speed, so the halo depth a channel needs grows with its speed. Velocity-dependent ragged halos exploit this, sending each channel only its own upwind depth. The 4D decomposition includes the toroidal angle, the 2D poloidal plane and the magnetic moment, and leaves the parallel velocity whole: no stage transports across the magnetic moment, while a parallel-velocity partition would put a halo inside the acceleration stage. On NVIDIA A100s the design strong-scales over the magnetic-moment axis from one to 12 four-GPU nodes, and a weak series of three points refining all five phase-space dimensions together, 16x in total per series point, holds a weak-scaling efficiency of 75% on 16 GPUs and 57% on 256, from a one-GPU base case. The pointwise interpolations within the poloidal plane do not conserve mass, and the full step's mass drift stays below 2.1x10^-5 at every series point. The design was implemented with an AI code assistant under a machine-checked regression discipline, in which every change is held to one of four exactness classes, from bit-identical to a physics rate within a stated tolerance.

</details>


### [116] [Polyconvex neural-network surrogates with learned paired directional features for anisotropic hyperelastic homogenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12801)
*S. Ares de Parga,A. Cornejo,J. A. Hernández,R. Rossi*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A polyconvex physics-augmented neural network (PANN) with paired directional features approximates anisotropic hyperelastic RVE responses while guaranteeing objectivity, stress-free reference, 2D polyconvexity, and growth conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Approximating effective anisotropic hyperelastic RVE responses requires constitutive expressiveness, mechanical consistency, and computational efficiency simultaneously, which standard models struggle to balance.

Method: PANN built on paired directional features with adaptable orientations, stretch powers, and determinant exponents, composed with convex nondecreasing cores (ICNN or ICKAN). Guarantees built in by construction; nonnegative energy certified separately.

Result: On multicavity RVE, learned features cut stress error sevenfold; six features suffice on both cells. Constrained energies stay under 1% stress error and displacement error in FE2 tensile-coupon application. Beyond data, the Unconstrained energy loses rank-one convexity and stops below half of the 500 MPa reached by constrained energies under confined compression.

Conclusion: The results quantify the accuracy cost of the guarantees and what they secure beyond the training data.

Abstract: Approximating the effective anisotropic hyperelastic response of a representative volume element (RVE) requires constitutive expressiveness, mechanical consistency, and computational efficiency. This work develops a polyconvex physics-augmented neural network (PANN) construction based on paired directional features whose orientations, stretch powers, and determinant exponents adapt to the RVE response. The features are composed with convex, componentwise nondecreasing cores, realized as an input-convex neural network (ICNN) or an input-convex Kolmogorov-Arnold network (ICKAN). The complete energies satisfy objectivity, an exactly stress-free reference, two-dimensional polyconvexity, and prescribed growth conditions by construction, and nonnegative energy is certified separately for the selected models. On a multicavity and a single-cavity periodic cell, these energies are compared with an Unconstrained energy, trained identically without the curvature and growth constraints, and with three hyperreduced projection-based reduced-order models (HPROMs), which serve as physics-based references. On the first cell, learning the features lowers the stress error sevenfold, and six features suffice on both. The constrained energies remain below one percent in stress error and in the displacement error of a tensile-coupon FE$^2$ application with a full-order reference, whereas the Unconstrained energy and the HPROMs are more accurate inside the data. Beyond the data, however, the Unconstrained energy loses rank-one convexity, and under confined compression it stops below half of the 500 MPa that the constrained energies reach. These results quantify what the guarantees cost in accuracy and what they secure beyond the data.

</details>


### [117] [Energy-dependent trapping of backscattered helium during magnetron sputtering growth: A molecular dynamics study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13509)
*Pascal Brault,Anne-Lise Thomann,Alejandro Borroto*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: MD simulations reveal the atomistic mechanism of helium trapping and bubble formation during magnetron sputtering of Al and Ti films, showing strong material- and energy-dependence of He incorporation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Helium incorporation during magnetron sputtering strongly affects thin film growth, enabling gas/solid nanocomposites and nanoporous structures, yet the atomistic trapping mechanism remains unclear.

Method: Molecular dynamics simulations of Al and Ti sputter deposition, accounting for kinetic energy distributions of sputtered metal atoms and backscattered gas species; compared with Ar, and analyzed via simulated thermal desorption spectroscopy (TDS).

Result: He is efficiently incorporated, forming high-pressure dense bubbles, with much higher He/metal ratio in Ti (~60%) than Al (~12%). Ti trapping is energy-dependent: negligible at thermal energies, rising from ~20 eV to saturation near 100 eV. TDS shows distinct release mechanisms: Al desorption at higher temperatures after surface Al removal; Ti desorption starts around 950 K forming porous structures. Ar shows negligible trapping.

Conclusion: Neither kinetic energy nor momentum alone explains strong He incorporation in Ti. Results agree with low-energy He ion implantation literature, clarifying atomistic conditions for noble gas trapping and offering a basis for controlling He incorporation and porosity in sputtered films.

Abstract: Helium incorporation during magnetron sputtering can strongly affect thin film growth and lead to the formation of gas/solid nanocomposites and nanoporous structures. Here, molecular dynamics (MD) simulations are used to investigate the trapping mechanism of helium atoms (He) and the subsequent formation of He bubbles during the sputter deposition of aluminum (Al) and titanium (Ti) thin films. The simulations account for the kinetic energy distributions of sputtered metal atoms and backscattered gas species. Results indicate that He is efficiently incorporated into the growing films, leading to the formation of high-pressure, dense He bubbles, with a much higher He-to-metal atomic ratio in Ti ($\sim$ 60%) than in Al ($\sim$ 12%). More importantly, the simulations reveal a clear energy dependence of He trapping in Ti: background He atoms at thermal energies are essentially not incorporated, whereas efficient trapping starts at an incident energy of approximately 20 eV and increases up to a saturation regime near 100 eV. Simulated thermal desorption spectroscopy (TDS) further reveals distinct release mechanisms in the two investigated metals: He desorption from Al occurs at higher temperatures and is preceded by the removal of surface Al atoms, whereas He and Ti atom desorption starts continuously around 950 K, leading to a porous structure. Comparative simulations with argon (Ar) demonstrate negligible trapping even at comparable kinetic energy or momentum, showing that neither parameter alone accounts for the strong He incorporation observed in Ti. The above results are in very good agreement with what is reported in literature for low-kinetic energy He ion implantation in metal surfaces, suggesting similar insertion mechanisms. They clarify the atomistic conditions governing noble gas trapping during film growth and provide a basis for controlling He incorporation and porosity in films synthesized by magnetron sputtering.

</details>


### [118] [All-electron RT-TDDFT with Exact Exchange based on Numeric Atom-centered Orbitals for Condensed Matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12896)
*Jianhang Xu,Christopher Shepard,Yosuke Kanai*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: An all-electron, numeric atom-centered orbital implementation of exact exchange for real-time TDDFT using a resolution-of-identity framework, enabling hybrid-XC functional simulations of molecular and periodic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RT-TDDFT is widely used for excited-state and nonequilibrium electron dynamics, and hybrid XC functionals with exact exchange are crucial for charge transfer and hot-carrier dynamics. However, the high cost of exact exchange has largely limited RT-TDDFT of condensed matter to semi-local functionals.

Method: The authors present an all-electron implementation of exact exchange for RT-TDDFT based on numeric atom-centered orbitals. They extend an efficient ground-state resolution-of-identity (RI) framework to the complex, time-dependent Hamiltonians needed for real-time propagation.

Result: Validation against an established RI approach for an isolated water molecule shows exact agreement for valence and core (K-edge) excitations. For crystalline silicon, the HSE06 spectrum reproduces prior real-time TDDFT results and matches experiment within ~0.2 eV, consistent with plane-wave pseudopotential implementations. For silicon carbide, valence and X-ray absorption spectra are computed at the Si L-edge and C K-edge.

Conclusion: These results establish an efficient, all-electron route to hybrid-XC functional RT-TDDFT for both molecular and condensed matter systems.

Abstract: Real-time time-dependent density functional theory (RT-TDDFT) has become a widely used first-principles method for studying excited-state and nonequilibrium electron dynamics. Hybrid exchange-correlation (XC) functionals, which incorporate a fraction of nonlocal exact exchange, are particularly important for accurately describing processes such as charge transfer and hot-carrier dynamics. However, the high computational cost which accompanies exact exchange, as required in hybrid XC functionals, has largely restricted RT-TDDFT simulations of condensed matter systems to using semi-local XC functionals. In this work, we present an all-electron implementation of exact exchange for RT-TDDFT based on numeric atom-centered orbitals, extending an efficient ground-state resolution-of-identity (RI) framework to the complex, time-dependent Hamiltonians required for real-time propagation. We validate the implementation against an established RI approach, for an isolated water molecule, finding exact agreement for both valence and core (K-edge) excitations. We also demonstrate the method on two extended periodic systems, crystalline silicon and silicon carbide. For silicon, the HSE06 spectrum reproduces previously reported real-time TDDFT results and agrees with experiment to within approximately 0.2~eV, consistent with plane-wave pseudopotential implementations. For silicon carbide, we compute valence and X-ray absorption spectra at the silicon $L$-edge and carbon $K$-edge. These results establish an efficient, all-electron route to hybrid-XC functional RT-TDDFT for both molecular and condensed matter systems.

</details>


### [119] [Physical-state supervision for design-only Gaussian process surrogates of implosion simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13822)
*Chang Liu,Hao Shen,Chuanying Li*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A physical-state-supervised Gaussian process surrogate learns simulation diagnostics from design variables, greatly reducing prediction error on implosion datasets at prediction time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulation diagnostics can capture sharply varying responses, but using them as surrogate inputs typically requires an extra calculation at prediction. The work seeks a way to leverage these physical diagnostics without that runtime cost.

Method: Cross-fitted state estimates augment a retained design representation. A global Gaussian process and an optional soft mixture of response-band experts share identical inputs. Evaluated on 1,987 paired 1D implosions and a 3,000-design Be/Cocktail set, with random-projection and shuffled-state controls plus a conditional state-error bound.

Result: Learned burn-weighted states (from calculations without alpha heating) cut mean squared log-response error by 60.25% (random holdout) and 52.98% (extrapolation). Controls do not reproduce gains. Experts help at small budgets on CH but little at 1,000 designs; Be/Cocktail shows weaker gains and some expert degradation.

Conclusion: Physical-state supervision is a useful representation for finite-data surrogates, while expert specialization should be chosen via validation. An open Python implementation, data, per-design results, and costs are released.

Abstract: Physical diagnostics recorded during simulation can organize a sharply varying response, but using them as surrogate inputs normally requires another calculation at prediction. We construct a physical-state-supervised Gaussian process surrogate that learns these diagnostics from design variables. Cross-fitted state estimates augment a retained design representation; a global Gaussian process and an optional soft mixture of response-band experts use identical inputs. On a database of 1,987 paired one-dimensional implosions, learned burn-weighted states from calculations without alpha-particle heating reduce mean squared log-response error by 60.25% and 52.98% for the global predictor on random holdout and design-space extrapolation. Random-projection and shuffled-state supervision do not reproduce these gains. For CH, matched-input comparisons show that experts help at smaller training budgets but contribute little at 1,000 designs with this representation. A thermal-feedback benchmark validates the complete learned-state procedure, while 3,000 newly calculated Be/Cocktail designs expose weaker extrapolation gains and cases where experts degrade accuracy. A conditional state-error bound separates encoding sensitivity from routing effects. An open Python implementation reproduces the archived predictions and is released with the data, per-design results, and measured costs. Physical-state supervision is thus a useful representation for finite-data surrogates, whereas expert specialization should be selected by validation.

</details>


### [120] [Alter-excitons in Altermagnets: Symmetry, Splitting and Dispersion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12900)
*Yuanfan Xiong,Liangyu Wang,Xuan Wu,Wei Hu,Jinlong Yang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Altermagnetic symmetry constrains electronic and excitonic excitations differently: symmetry-protected band degeneracies can be intrinsically lifted in the exciton spectrum, yielding a new class of spin-split quasiparticles called "alter-excitons" without spin-orbit coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spin-space-group (SSG) theory recently identified altermagnets as nonrelativistic collinear magnets with unconventional spin-dependent electronic structures. However, how altermagnetic symmetry acts on two-body (excitonic) excitations, and whether it matches its effect on quasiparticle bands, was unexplored.

Method: Combined symmetry analysis with many-body perturbation theory (BSE-type treatment) to compare the SSG symmetry of quasiparticle and two-body Hamiltonians and to predict exciton dispersions.

Result: Despite identical SSG symmetries of the quasiparticle and two-body Hamiltonians, symmetry-protected degeneracies in the electronic band structure are intrinsically lifted in the exciton spectrum at specific momenta. This splitting stems solely from the many-body nature of excitons, requiring neither spin-orbit coupling nor noncollinear magnetic order. A novel class of spin-split excitonic quasiparticles, termed alter-excitons, is predicted with characteristic dispersions.

Conclusion: Excitons serve as a sensitive probe of hidden spin-space symmetries, opening a new route toward symmetry-engineered many-body phenomena in collinear magnets.

Abstract: Magnetism provides a fertile platform for discovering emergent quantum phenomena governed by symmetry. Recent developments of spin-space-group (SSG) theory have revealed altermagnets as a distinct class of nonrelativistic collinear magnets with unconventional spin-dependent electronic structures. Here, we demonstrate that altermagnetic symmetry imposes fundamentally different constraints on electronic and excitonic excitations. Although the quasiparticle and two-body Hamiltonians possess identical SSG symmetries, we find that symmetry-protected degeneracies present in the electronic band structure can be intrinsically lifted in the exciton spectrum at specific momenta. This splitting arises solely from the many-body nature of excitons and does not require spin-orbit coupling or noncollinear magnetic order. Combining symmetry analysis with many-body perturbation theory, we predict a novel class of spin-split excitonic quasiparticles, termed alter-excitons, and reveal their characteristic dispersions in altermagnetic systems. Our results establish excitons as a sensitive probe of hidden spin-space symmetries and uncover a new route toward symmetry-engineered many-body phenomena in collinear magnets.

</details>


### [121] [Parallel Design of a GPU-Resident Semi-Lagrangian Drift-Kinetic Solver on Unstructured Extruded Tokamak Meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12585)
*Mark F. Adams*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A parallel/GPU design for a 3X+2V semi-Lagrangian DG drift-kinetic tokamak solver using velocity-dependent ragged halos and a 4D decomposition, achieving 75% weak-scaling efficiency on 16 GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semi-Lagrangian schemes are attractive for drift-kinetic tokamak simulation (unconditionally stable, exact mass conservation with DG), but their parallelization is challenging: transport follows magnetic field lines across flux-surface-aligned poloidal planes, and each velocity channel advances a different distance per step, so halo requirements grow with parallel speed. A fixed halo wastes communication, motivating a velocity-aware parallel/GPU design.

Method: The solver advects a 3X+2V drift-kinetic distribution on unstructured, flux-surface-aligned poloidal planes of shaped cross section, extruded toroidally. Velocity-dependent ragged halos send each velocity channel only its own upwind depth, proportional to its parallel speed. The 4D decomposition spans toroidal angle, the 2D poloidal plane, and magnetic moment, keeping parallel velocity whole so no stage transports across magnetic moment (which would place a halo inside the acceleration stage). Implemented with an AI code assistant under a machine-checked regression discipline with four exactness classes.

Result: On NVIDIA A100s, strong-scaling over the magnetic-moment axis from 1 to 12 four-GPU nodes; a three-point weak series refining all five phase-space dimensions 16x per point holds 75% efficiency on 16 GPUs and 57% on 256, from a one-GPU base case. Full-step mass drift stays below 2.1x10^-5 at every series point (pointwise poloidal interpolations do not conserve mass, but overall drift remains small).

Conclusion: The velocity-dependent ragged-halo, 4D-decomposed GPU design scales well and preserves mass to within tight tolerances, showing that SLDG drift-kinetic solvers can be effectively parallelized on modern GPUs with rigorous regression discipline.

Abstract: Semi-Lagrangian (SL) schemes advect a distribution function along characteristics and remap it to a fixed grid. They are unconditionally stable without a global solve, and with discontinuous Galerkin (DG) integration the transfer along a magnetic field line conserves mass exactly. This paper presents the parallel and GPU design of a 3X+2V SLDG drift-kinetic tokamak solver on unstructured, flux-surface-aligned poloidal planes of shaped cross section, extruded toroidally. Transport follows magnetic field lines across the planes, and each velocity channel advances a fixed distance per step, proportional to its parallel speed, so the halo depth a channel needs grows with its speed. Velocity-dependent ragged halos exploit this, sending each channel only its own upwind depth. The 4D decomposition includes the toroidal angle, the 2D poloidal plane and the magnetic moment, and leaves the parallel velocity whole: no stage transports across the magnetic moment, while a parallel-velocity partition would put a halo inside the acceleration stage. On NVIDIA A100s the design strong-scales over the magnetic-moment axis from one to 12 four-GPU nodes, and a weak series of three points refining all five phase-space dimensions together, 16x in total per series point, holds a weak-scaling efficiency of 75% on 16 GPUs and 57% on 256, from a one-GPU base case. The pointwise interpolations within the poloidal plane do not conserve mass, and the full step's mass drift stays below 2.1x10^-5 at every series point. The design was implemented with an AI code assistant under a machine-checked regression discipline, in which every change is held to one of four exactness classes, from bit-identical to a physics rate within a stated tolerance.

</details>


### [122] [Polyconvex neural-network surrogates with learned paired directional features for anisotropic hyperelastic homogenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12801)
*S. Ares de Parga,A. Cornejo,J. A. Hernández,R. Rossi*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A polyconvex physics-augmented neural network (PANN) with paired, adaptable directional features and convex cores (ICNN/ICKAN) approximates anisotropic hyperelastic RVE response while guaranteeing objectivity, stress-free reference, 2D polyconvexity, and growth; it matches HPROM accuracy and secures stability/extrapolation beyond data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Approximating an RVE's effective anisotropic hyperelastic response demands constitutive expressiveness, mechanical consistency, and computational efficiency, which are hard to satisfy simultaneously.

Method: Build a polyconvex PANN from paired directional features (adaptable orientations, stretch powers, determinant exponents) composed with convex componentwise nondecreasing cores (ICNN or ICKAN), enforcing objectivity, exact stress-free reference, 2D polyconvexity, prescribed growth, plus certified nonnegative energy. Compare against an Unconstrained energy and three hyperreduced projection-based ROMs on multicavity and single-cavity periodic cells.

Result: Learning features cuts stress error sevenfold on the first cell; six features suffice on both. Constrained energies stay under 1% stress error and tensile-coupon FE² displacement error versus full-order reference, while Unconstrained energy and HPROMs are more accurate inside the data. Beyond it, Unconstrained energy loses rank-one convexity and, under confined compression, stalls below half of the 500 MPa reached by constrained energies.

Conclusion: The construction quantifies the accuracy cost of the guarantees and the robustness they secure outside the training data.

Abstract: Approximating the effective anisotropic hyperelastic response of a representative volume element (RVE) requires constitutive expressiveness, mechanical consistency, and computational efficiency. This work develops a polyconvex physics-augmented neural network (PANN) construction based on paired directional features whose orientations, stretch powers, and determinant exponents adapt to the RVE response. The features are composed with convex, componentwise nondecreasing cores, realized as an input-convex neural network (ICNN) or an input-convex Kolmogorov-Arnold network (ICKAN). The complete energies satisfy objectivity, an exactly stress-free reference, two-dimensional polyconvexity, and prescribed growth conditions by construction, and nonnegative energy is certified separately for the selected models. On a multicavity and a single-cavity periodic cell, these energies are compared with an Unconstrained energy, trained identically without the curvature and growth constraints, and with three hyperreduced projection-based reduced-order models (HPROMs), which serve as physics-based references. On the first cell, learning the features lowers the stress error sevenfold, and six features suffice on both. The constrained energies remain below one percent in stress error and in the displacement error of a tensile-coupon FE$^2$ application with a full-order reference, whereas the Unconstrained energy and the HPROMs are more accurate inside the data. Beyond the data, however, the Unconstrained energy loses rank-one convexity, and under confined compression it stops below half of the 500 MPa that the constrained energies reach. These results quantify what the guarantees cost in accuracy and what they secure beyond the data.

</details>


### [123] [Energy-dependent trapping of backscattered helium during magnetron sputtering growth: A molecular dynamics study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13509)
*Pascal Brault,Anne-Lise Thomann,Alejandro Borroto*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: MD simulations reveal how helium is trapped and forms high-pressure bubbles during magnetron sputtering of Al and Ti films, with much stronger incorporation in Ti (~60%) than Al (~12%), showing a clear energy-dependent trapping threshold near 20 eV in Ti.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Helium incorporation during magnetron sputtering strongly affects thin film growth and can produce gas/solid nanocomposites and nanoporous structures, yet the atomistic trapping mechanism and conditions governing it are not well understood.

Method: Molecular dynamics simulations of He trapping and bubble formation during sputter deposition of Al and Ti films, accounting for kinetic energy distributions of sputtered metal atoms and backscattered gas species. Simulated thermal desorption spectroscopy (TDS) and comparative Ar simulations were also performed.

Result: He is efficiently incorporated, forming high-pressure dense bubbles, with He-to-metal ratio ~60% in Ti versus ~12% in Al. In Ti, trapping shows clear energy dependence: negligible at thermal energies, onset near 20 eV, saturating near 100 eV. TDS reveals distinct release mechanisms: He desorption from Al at higher temperatures preceded by surface Al removal, while He and Ti desorb continuously around 950 K, forming a porous structure. Ar simulations show negligible trapping even at comparable energy or momentum.

Conclusion: Results agree well with literature on low-kinetic-energy He ion implantation in metals, suggesting similar insertion mechanisms. The findings clarify atomistic conditions governing noble gas trapping during film growth and provide a basis for controlling He incorporation and porosity in magnetron-sputtered films.

Abstract: Helium incorporation during magnetron sputtering can strongly affect thin film growth and lead to the formation of gas/solid nanocomposites and nanoporous structures. Here, molecular dynamics (MD) simulations are used to investigate the trapping mechanism of helium atoms (He) and the subsequent formation of He bubbles during the sputter deposition of aluminum (Al) and titanium (Ti) thin films. The simulations account for the kinetic energy distributions of sputtered metal atoms and backscattered gas species. Results indicate that He is efficiently incorporated into the growing films, leading to the formation of high-pressure, dense He bubbles, with a much higher He-to-metal atomic ratio in Ti ($\sim$ 60%) than in Al ($\sim$ 12%). More importantly, the simulations reveal a clear energy dependence of He trapping in Ti: background He atoms at thermal energies are essentially not incorporated, whereas efficient trapping starts at an incident energy of approximately 20 eV and increases up to a saturation regime near 100 eV. Simulated thermal desorption spectroscopy (TDS) further reveals distinct release mechanisms in the two investigated metals: He desorption from Al occurs at higher temperatures and is preceded by the removal of surface Al atoms, whereas He and Ti atom desorption starts continuously around 950 K, leading to a porous structure. Comparative simulations with argon (Ar) demonstrate negligible trapping even at comparable kinetic energy or momentum, showing that neither parameter alone accounts for the strong He incorporation observed in Ti. The above results are in very good agreement with what is reported in literature for low-kinetic energy He ion implantation in metal surfaces, suggesting similar insertion mechanisms. They clarify the atomistic conditions governing noble gas trapping during film growth and provide a basis for controlling He incorporation and porosity in films synthesized by magnetron sputtering.

</details>


### [124] [All-electron RT-TDDFT with Exact Exchange based on Numeric Atom-centered Orbitals for Condensed Matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12896)
*Jianhang Xu,Christopher Shepard,Yosuke Kanai*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents an all-electron implementation of exact exchange for real-time TDDFT using numeric atom-centered orbitals, extending ground-state RI methods to time-dependent hybrid-functional simulations for molecules and solids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hybrid XC functionals are essential for accurately describing charge transfer and hot-carrier dynamics, but the high cost of exact exchange has restricted RT-TDDFT simulations of condensed matter to semi-local functionals.

Method: An all-electron exact-exchange RT-TDDFT implementation based on numeric atom-centered orbitals, extending an efficient ground-state resolution-of-identity (RI) framework to the complex time-dependent Hamiltonians needed for real-time propagation.

Result: Validated against an established RI approach for an isolated water molecule with exact agreement for valence and core (K-edge) excitations. For silicon, the HSE06 spectrum reproduces prior real-time TDDFT results and agrees with experiment within ~0.2 eV. For silicon carbide, valence and X-ray absorption spectra at the Si L-edge and C K-edge are computed.

Conclusion: The work establishes an efficient, all-electron route to hybrid-XC-functional RT-TDDFT for both molecular and condensed matter systems.

Abstract: Real-time time-dependent density functional theory (RT-TDDFT) has become a widely used first-principles method for studying excited-state and nonequilibrium electron dynamics. Hybrid exchange-correlation (XC) functionals, which incorporate a fraction of nonlocal exact exchange, are particularly important for accurately describing processes such as charge transfer and hot-carrier dynamics. However, the high computational cost which accompanies exact exchange, as required in hybrid XC functionals, has largely restricted RT-TDDFT simulations of condensed matter systems to using semi-local XC functionals. In this work, we present an all-electron implementation of exact exchange for RT-TDDFT based on numeric atom-centered orbitals, extending an efficient ground-state resolution-of-identity (RI) framework to the complex, time-dependent Hamiltonians required for real-time propagation. We validate the implementation against an established RI approach, for an isolated water molecule, finding exact agreement for both valence and core (K-edge) excitations. We also demonstrate the method on two extended periodic systems, crystalline silicon and silicon carbide. For silicon, the HSE06 spectrum reproduces previously reported real-time TDDFT results and agrees with experiment to within approximately 0.2~eV, consistent with plane-wave pseudopotential implementations. For silicon carbide, we compute valence and X-ray absorption spectra at the silicon $L$-edge and carbon $K$-edge. These results establish an efficient, all-electron route to hybrid-XC functional RT-TDDFT for both molecular and condensed matter systems.

</details>


### [125] [Physical-state supervision for design-only Gaussian process surrogates of implosion simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13822)
*Chang Liu,Hao Shen,Chuanying Li*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a physical-state-supervised Gaussian process surrogate that learns simulation diagnostics from design variables to improve prediction of sharply varying responses for one-dimensional implosions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulation diagnostics capture sharp response variation but normally require recalculation at prediction time, making them impractical as surrogate inputs.

Method: A Gaussian process surrogate learns burn-weighted physical states from design variables. Cross-fitted state estimates augment a retained design representation, feeding a global GP and an optional soft mixture of response-band experts. Random-projection, shuffled-state, and matched-input controls test the mechanism.

Result: Learned states reduce mean squared log-response error by 60.25% (random holdout) and 52.98% (extrapolation) versus baselines; controls do not reproduce gains. Experts help at small budgets but contribute little at 1,000 designs. Be/Cocktail tests show weaker extrapolation gains and occasional expert degradation.

Conclusion: Physical-state supervision is a useful representation for finite-data surrogates, while expert specialization should be chosen via validation. A conditional state-error bound separates encoding sensitivity from routing effects; an open implementation, data, and results are released.

Abstract: Physical diagnostics recorded during simulation can organize a sharply varying response, but using them as surrogate inputs normally requires another calculation at prediction. We construct a physical-state-supervised Gaussian process surrogate that learns these diagnostics from design variables. Cross-fitted state estimates augment a retained design representation; a global Gaussian process and an optional soft mixture of response-band experts use identical inputs. On a database of 1,987 paired one-dimensional implosions, learned burn-weighted states from calculations without alpha-particle heating reduce mean squared log-response error by 60.25% and 52.98% for the global predictor on random holdout and design-space extrapolation. Random-projection and shuffled-state supervision do not reproduce these gains. For CH, matched-input comparisons show that experts help at smaller training budgets but contribute little at 1,000 designs with this representation. A thermal-feedback benchmark validates the complete learned-state procedure, while 3,000 newly calculated Be/Cocktail designs expose weaker extrapolation gains and cases where experts degrade accuracy. A conditional state-error bound separates encoding sensitivity from routing effects. An open Python implementation reproduces the archived predictions and is released with the data, per-design results, and measured costs. Physical-state supervision is thus a useful representation for finite-data surrogates, whereas expert specialization should be selected by validation.

</details>


### [126] [Alter-excitons in Altermagnets: Symmetry, Splitting and Dispersion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12900)
*Yuanfan Xiong,Liangyu Wang,Xuan Wu,Wei Hu,Jinlong Yang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces "alter-excitons," a class of spin-split excitonic quasiparticles in altermagnets, showing that altermagnetic symmetry lifts electronic degeneracies in the exciton spectrum purely through many-body effects, without spin-orbit coupling or noncollinear order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spin-space-group theory recently established altermagnets as collinear magnets with unconventional spin-dependent electronic structures. However, whether and how altermagnetic symmetry constrains excitonic (two-body) excitations—as opposed to single-particle electronic ones—remained unexplored.

Method: The authors combine spin-space-group symmetry analysis with many-body perturbation theory to compare the symmetries of quasiparticle and two-body Hamiltonians and to compute the exciton spectrum in altermagnetic systems.

Result: Although the quasiparticle and two-body Hamiltonians share identical SSG symmetries, symmetry-protected degeneracies in the electronic band structure are intrinsically lifted in the exciton spectrum at specific momenta. This splitting originates solely from the many-body nature of excitons—requiring neither spin-orbit coupling nor noncollinear magnetic order. The authors predict spin-split excitonic quasiparticles (alter-excitons) with characteristic dispersions.

Conclusion: Excitons serve as a sensitive probe of hidden spin-space symmetries and open a new route toward symmetry-engineered many-body phenomena in collinear magnets.

Abstract: Magnetism provides a fertile platform for discovering emergent quantum phenomena governed by symmetry. Recent developments of spin-space-group (SSG) theory have revealed altermagnets as a distinct class of nonrelativistic collinear magnets with unconventional spin-dependent electronic structures. Here, we demonstrate that altermagnetic symmetry imposes fundamentally different constraints on electronic and excitonic excitations. Although the quasiparticle and two-body Hamiltonians possess identical SSG symmetries, we find that symmetry-protected degeneracies present in the electronic band structure can be intrinsically lifted in the exciton spectrum at specific momenta. This splitting arises solely from the many-body nature of excitons and does not require spin-orbit coupling or noncollinear magnetic order. Combining symmetry analysis with many-body perturbation theory, we predict a novel class of spin-split excitonic quasiparticles, termed alter-excitons, and reveal their characteristic dispersions in altermagnetic systems. Our results establish excitons as a sensitive probe of hidden spin-space symmetries and uncover a new route toward symmetry-engineered many-body phenomena in collinear magnets.

</details>

### [127] [Implementation and verification of a new free boundary module for Resistive Wall Modes simulations with the nonlinear 3D full MHD code SpeCyl](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13428)
*Francesco Galeazzi,Daniele Bonfiglio,Luca Spinicci,Susanna Cappello*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: SpeCyl, a nonlinear 3D full MHD code, is upgraded with double resistive wall thin shell boundary conditions and a free-flow edge plasma velocity treatment, then verified against Shafranov's analytic theory and validated against RFX-mod experiments for RWM and external kink growth rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Free boundary instabilities like resistive wall modes (RWMs) and external kink modes are critical for magnetic fusion reactors, so self-consistent numerical simulations of these modes require accurate magnetic boundary conditions and edge plasma treatment.

Method: A new implementation in the nonlinear 3D full MHD code SpeCyl self-consistently combines a free-flow 3D edge plasma velocity treatment with double resistive wall thin shell magnetic boundary conditions. Two coaxial resistive shells surround the plasma with vacuum between and outside them, and their positions and time constants can be varied. The implementation is verified against Shafranov's analytic theory for a circular, large aspect ratio tokamak with flat current profile.

Result: The new boundary conditions show good agreement for linear growth rates of the (m=2,n=1) RWM and external kink over a wide range of wall positions, shell time constants, and reference equilibria. RWM growth rates are benchmarked against previous RWM modeling and validated against RFX-mod tokamak plasmas.

Conclusion: The new boundary conditions make SpeCyl a flexible and robust tool for investigating free boundary MHD modes in large aspect ratio magnetic confinement configurations, such as RFX-mod and the expected RFX-mod2.

Abstract: Self-consistent numerical simulations of free boundary instabilities, such as resistive wall modes (RWMs) and external kink modes, require special attention given their relevance on the path towards magnetic fusion based nuclear reactors. In this work, a new implementation and a verification benchmark are presented for the nonlinear three-dimensional (3D) full magneto-hydrodynamics (MHD) code SpeCyl, which self-consistently combines a free flow 3D treatment for the edge plasma velocity with double resistive wall thin shell magnetic boundary conditions, capable of capturing the essential features of common setups in experimental devices. Two coaxial resistive shells surround the plasma. Vacuum conditions are assumed between and outside the resistive shells, whose relative positions and time constants can be varied on purpose.
  The implementation is extensively verified against Shafranov's analytic theory, valid for a circular cross section large aspect ratio tokamak and assuming a flat current profile. The correctness of newly implemented Boundary Conditions (BCs) is confirmed by the good agreement obtained for the linear growth rates of (m=2,n=1) RWM and external kink, over a wide range of wall positions, resistive shell time constants, and for different initial reference equilibria. The (m=2,n=1) RWM growth rates are also benchmarked against previous different RWMs modelling and successful validation studies for RFX-mod tokamak plasmas. The new BCs make SpeCyl a flexible and robust tool to investigate free boundary MHD modes in large aspect ratio magnetic confinement configurations, such as those achieved with the RFX-mod machine and expected in RFX-mod2.

</details>


### [128] [Implementation and verification of a new free boundary module for Resistive Wall Modes simulations with the nonlinear 3D full MHD code SpeCyl](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13428)
*Francesco Galeazzi,Daniele Bonfiglio,Luca Spinicci,Susanna Cappello*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A new implementation and verification benchmark for the nonlinear 3D full MHD code SpeCyl is presented, combining a free-edge plasma flow treatment with double resistive wall thin shell boundary conditions, verified against Shafranov's analytic theory for RWM and external kink growth rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-consistent numerical simulations of free boundary instabilities like RWMs and external kink modes are essential for magnetic fusion reactors. Existing tools need accurate, flexible magnetic boundary conditions capturing realistic experimental setups (e.g., double resistive shells) to properly model these modes.

Method: The SpeCyl code self-consistently couples a free 3D edge plasma velocity flow treatment with double resistive wall thin shell magnetic boundary conditions. Two coaxial resistive shells surround the plasma with vacuum regions between and outside; their positions and time constants are variable. The implementation is verified against Shafranov's analytic theory (circular cross-section, large aspect ratio, flat current profile).

Result: Good agreement is obtained for linear growth rates of (m=2,n=1) RWM and external kink across a wide range of wall positions, shell time constants, and initial equilibria. RWM growth rates are also benchmarked against previous modelling and validated with RFX-mod tokamak plasmas.

Conclusion: The new boundary conditions make SpeCyl a flexible, robust tool for investigating free boundary MHD modes in large aspect ratio confinement configurations such as RFX-mod and RFX-mod2.

Abstract: Self-consistent numerical simulations of free boundary instabilities, such as resistive wall modes (RWMs) and external kink modes, require special attention given their relevance on the path towards magnetic fusion based nuclear reactors. In this work, a new implementation and a verification benchmark are presented for the nonlinear three-dimensional (3D) full magneto-hydrodynamics (MHD) code SpeCyl, which self-consistently combines a free flow 3D treatment for the edge plasma velocity with double resistive wall thin shell magnetic boundary conditions, capable of capturing the essential features of common setups in experimental devices. Two coaxial resistive shells surround the plasma. Vacuum conditions are assumed between and outside the resistive shells, whose relative positions and time constants can be varied on purpose.
  The implementation is extensively verified against Shafranov's analytic theory, valid for a circular cross section large aspect ratio tokamak and assuming a flat current profile. The correctness of newly implemented Boundary Conditions (BCs) is confirmed by the good agreement obtained for the linear growth rates of (m=2,n=1) RWM and external kink, over a wide range of wall positions, resistive shell time constants, and for different initial reference equilibria. The (m=2,n=1) RWM growth rates are also benchmarked against previous different RWMs modelling and successful validation studies for RFX-mod tokamak plasmas. The new BCs make SpeCyl a flexible and robust tool to investigate free boundary MHD modes in large aspect ratio magnetic confinement configurations, such as those achieved with the RFX-mod machine and expected in RFX-mod2.

</details>

# math.CA [[Back]](#toc)

### [129] [A higher-dimensional Schrödinger maximal estimate along families of bi-Lipschitz curves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12565)
*Javier Minguillón*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: The paper proves sharp maximal estimates for Schrödinger operators along a family of curves parametrized by a fractal set Θ, achieving thresholds that depend on the upper Minkowski dimension β of Θ.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish L^2 maximal estimates for the Schrödinger equation restricted to curves associated with fractal sets of dimension β, extending classical results to fractal settings and identifying sharp regularity thresholds.

Method: The proof uses a fractal L^2 estimate of Du–Zhang, temporal localization of Lee, the locally constant property, and a counting argument exploiting the Lipschitz properties of the curve γ in its last two variables.

Result: For 0 ≤ β ≤ n-1, the maximal estimate holds for every s > n(β+1)/(2(n+1)). For n-1 < β ≤ n, the threshold is s > min{β/2 + 1/(2(n+1)), n/2}.

Conclusion: The results provide sharp fractal-dependent Sobolev regularity thresholds for Schrödinger maximal estimates along bi-Lipschitz/Lipschitz families of curves, unifying and extending prior Euclidean maximal estimates.

Abstract: Let $Θ\subset\mathbb{R}^n$, $n\geq 1$ be compact with upper Minkowski dimension $β$, and let $γ(x,t,θ)$ be a family of curves which is bi-Lipschitz in the first variable and Lipschitz in the last two. We prove the maximal estimate $$\left\|\sup_{(τ,θ)\in[0,1)\timesΘ}\left|e^{iτΔ}f(γ(\,\cdot\,,τ,θ))\right|\right\|_{L^2(B^n_1)}\lesssim_s\|f\|_{H^s(\mathbb{R}^n)},$$ for every $s>\tfrac{n(β+1)}{2(n+1)}$, in the range $0\leβ\le n-1$. In the range $n-1<β\leq n$ we prove the threshold $s>\min\big\lbrace\fracβ2 + \frac 1{2(n+1)},\frac n2\big\rbrace$. The proof relies on a fractal $L^2$ estimate of Du--Zhang. We also use the temporal localization of Lee, the locally constant property, and a counting argument allowed by the Lipschitz properties of the curve.

</details>


### [130] [Characterizing higher-dimensional Hölder domains via spherical geometric functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13866)
*Emily Casey,Max Engelstein,Tatiana Toro*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: The paper characterizes domain regularity via the Hölder decay of a spherical spectral function, showing it is equivalent to complementary domains being separated by a C^{1,β} manifold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extends prior analyses to dimensions ≥3 and to regularity above the continuous threshold, addressing a question of Fleschler–Tolsa–Villa.

Method: Introduces a spherical analogue of Jones β-numbers and relates their decay to geometric regularity of the domain.

Result: The spherical spectral function has Hölder decay iff the domains are complementary and separated by a C^{1,β}-manifold; decay of spherical β-numbers implies geometric regularity.

Conclusion: Links spectral behavior to domain regularity, providing an independent-interest result that partially answers FTV23's Question 1.

Abstract: In this paper we characterize the regularity of a domain in terms of the behavior of a certain spherical function. More precisely, given two domains we show that a certain spherical spectral function (first defined in \cite{FTV23}, see also \cite{AKN21}) has Hölder decay if and only if the domains are complementary and separated by a $C^{1,β}$-manifold. This extends the analysis in \cite{Ca24} to dimensions $\geq 3$ and in \cite{FTV23, CTV25} to regularity above the continuous threshold.
  Along the way, we introduce a spherical analogue of the Jones $β$-numbers and show that the decay of these numbers implies geometric regularity of the domain on which they are defined. We believe this result is of independent interest and partially addresses a question of Fleschler-Tolsa-Villa \cite[Question 1]{FTV23}.

</details>


### [131] [A higher-dimensional Schrödinger maximal estimate along families of bi-Lipschitz curves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12565)
*Javier Minguillón*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: The paper proves sharp $L^2$ maximal estimates for solutions to the Schrödinger equation restricted to a fractal family of curves, with thresholds depending on the upper Minkowski dimension $\beta$ of the curve family.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maximal estimates for the Schrödinger operator along curves are central in harmonic analysis and PDEs. Prior work focused mainly on smooth or non-fractal curves; extending these to fractal sets governed by upper Minkowski dimension $\beta$ requires new tools.

Method: The proof combines a fractal $L^2$ estimate of Du--Zhang, temporal localization due to Lee, the locally constant property, and a counting argument that exploits the Lipschitz properties of the curve. The curve family $\gamma(x,t,\theta)$ is assumed bi-Lipschitz in the first variable and Lipschitz in the last two.

Result: For $s > \frac{n(\beta+1)}{2(n+1)}$ in the range $0\le\beta\le n-1$, the maximal estimate holds. For $n-1<\beta\le n$, the threshold becomes $s>\min\{\frac{\beta}{2}+\frac{1}{2(n+1)},\frac{n}{2}\}$.

Conclusion: The paper establishes Sobolev-space maximal bounds for the Schrödinger flow along fractal curve families, giving dimension-dependent thresholds that extend classical Euclidean results to the fractal setting.

Abstract: Let $Θ\subset\mathbb{R}^n$, $n\geq 1$ be compact with upper Minkowski dimension $β$, and let $γ(x,t,θ)$ be a family of curves which is bi-Lipschitz in the first variable and Lipschitz in the last two. We prove the maximal estimate $$\left\|\sup_{(τ,θ)\in[0,1)\timesΘ}\left|e^{iτΔ}f(γ(\,\cdot\,,τ,θ))\right|\right\|_{L^2(B^n_1)}\lesssim_s\|f\|_{H^s(\mathbb{R}^n)},$$ for every $s>\tfrac{n(β+1)}{2(n+1)}$, in the range $0\leβ\le n-1$. In the range $n-1<β\leq n$ we prove the threshold $s>\min\big\lbrace\fracβ2 + \frac 1{2(n+1)},\frac n2\big\rbrace$. The proof relies on a fractal $L^2$ estimate of Du--Zhang. We also use the temporal localization of Lee, the locally constant property, and a counting argument allowed by the Lipschitz properties of the curve.

</details>


### [132] [Characterizing higher-dimensional Hölder domains via spherical geometric functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13866)
*Emily Casey,Max Engelstein,Tatiana Toro*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: The paper links the Hölder decay of a spherical spectral function to the C^{1,β} regularity of complementary domains, extending prior work to higher dimensions and above the continuous threshold, and introduces spherical Jones β-numbers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior results (Ca24, FTV23, CTV25) only covered limited dimensions or regularity up to the continuous threshold. The authors aim to generalize the characterization of domain regularity via a spherical spectral function to dimensions ≥3 and to higher regularity, partly answering a question of Fleschler-Tolsa-Villa.

Method: They relate the Hölder decay of a spherical spectral function to the geometry of domains, showing the decay holds iff the domains are complementary and separated by a C^{1,β}-manifold. They also introduce a spherical analogue of Jones β-numbers and prove that decay of these numbers implies geometric regularity of the domain.

Result: The spherical spectral function has Hölder decay if and only if the two domains are complementary and separated by a C^{1,β}-manifold. This extends results to dimensions ≥3 and regularity beyond the continuous threshold. Decay of the new spherical Jones β-numbers implies geometric regularity.

Conclusion: Regularity of a domain can be characterized through spectral decay, and the spherical β-numbers provide an independent tool for detecting geometric regularity, partially resolving a question of Fleschler-Tolsa-Villa.

Abstract: In this paper we characterize the regularity of a domain in terms of the behavior of a certain spherical function. More precisely, given two domains we show that a certain spherical spectral function (first defined in \cite{FTV23}, see also \cite{AKN21}) has Hölder decay if and only if the domains are complementary and separated by a $C^{1,β}$-manifold. This extends the analysis in \cite{Ca24} to dimensions $\geq 3$ and in \cite{FTV23, CTV25} to regularity above the continuous threshold.
  Along the way, we introduce a spherical analogue of the Jones $β$-numbers and show that the decay of these numbers implies geometric regularity of the domain on which they are defined. We believe this result is of independent interest and partially addresses a question of Fleschler-Tolsa-Villa \cite[Question 1]{FTV23}.

</details>

### [133] [Block encoding of semiseparable matrices via their generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13228)
*Giacomo Antonioli,Gianna M. Del Corso,Roel Van Beeumen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: An explicit polylog-depth quantum circuit block-encodes an N×N generator-representable semiseparable (one-pair) matrix directly from its two generators using 2n+2 ancillas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Block encodings are key primitives in quantum algorithms (e.g., for Hamiltonian simulation and matrix arithmetic), but constructing them for structured matrices is often costly. Semiseparable (one-pair) matrices are a useful structured class, and a direct construction from their compact generators is desirable.

Method: Build an explicit quantum circuit from the two generators of a generator-representable semiseparable N×N matrix (N=2^n). Use 2n+2 ancilla qubits and primitives—cyclic shifts, swaps, and Hadamard gates—efficiently implemented via binary decomposition of indices. Generator loading uses state-preparation routines.

Result: The circuit has O(polylog N) depth and realizes a (2√N, 2n+2, 2√2 ε)-block encoding, where ε depends on the accuracy of the state-preparation routines loading the generators.

Conclusion: Generator-representable semiseparable matrices admit explicit, low-depth quantum block encodings constructed directly from their generators, enabling efficient use as primitives in quantum algorithms.

Abstract: We present an explicit quantum circuit that constructs a block encoding of a generator-representable semiseparable (one-pair) matrix directly from its two generators. For an $N\times N$ semiseparable matrix with $N=2^{n}$, the circuit uses $2n+2$ ancilla qubits and has $O({\rm polylog} N)$ depth, and it realizes a $(2\sqrt{N},\,2n+2,\,2 \sqrt{2}\ \varepsilon)$-block encoding, where $\varepsilon$ is controlled by the accuracy of the state-preparation routines that load the generators. The building blocks of the circuit are cyclic shifts, swaps, and Hadamard gates, each admitting an efficient implementation through the binary decomposition of the relevant indices.

</details>


### [134] [Encoding Matters: A Qubit-Efficient Reformulation for Quantum Resilient Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13621)
*Emanuele G. Dalla Torre,Bat-El Friedman,Daniel Porat*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work introduces a structure-aware encoding reduction that maps logistics and routing problems onto fewer qubits, demonstrated on latency-resilient routing with dual-link failures, achieving 100X higher success probability on a trapped-ion quantum computer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Each additional qubit doubles the state space but also increases circuit size, constraint overhead, and hardware noise exposure. This tradeoff is a central practical concern for near-term quantum computers, making qubit count a critical issue.

Method: A structure-aware encoding reduction that efficiently maps logistics and routing problems onto qubits, applied to a benchmark of latency-resilient routing under dual-link failures, then executed on a trapped-ion quantum computer.

Result: The reduced formulation substantially lowers both qubit count and compiled circuit complexity while preserving the original optimization task, yielding a 100X improvement in success probability on the same trapped-ion quantum computer as the benchmark.

Conclusion: Careful problem formulation can make current quantum hardware substantially more effective for practical optimization tasks.

Abstract: Qubits are powerful computational resources because each additional qubit doubles the size of the accessible state space. This expressive power comes at a cost: adding qubits also increases circuit size, constraint overhead, and exposure to hardware noise. These limitations are especially critical for near-term quantum computers, making the number of qubits required to formulate a problem a central practical concern. In this work, we present a method for efficiently mapping logistics and routing problems onto qubits through structure-aware encoding reduction. We demonstrate the method on a benchmark problem of latency-resilient routing under dual-link failures. For this benchmark, the reduced formulation substantially lowers both the qubit count and the compiled circuit complexity while preserving the original optimization task. By running the reduced model on the same trapped-ion quantum computer as the benchmark, we obtain a 100X improvement in the success probability. This finding shows how careful problem formulation can make current quantum hardware substantially more effective for practical optimization.

</details>


### [135] [Block encoding of semiseparable matrices via their generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13228)
*Giacomo Antonioli,Gianna M. Del Corso,Roel Van Beeumen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: An explicit quantum circuit block-encodes a generator-representable semiseparable (one-pair) matrix directly from its two generators using 2n+2 ancilla qubits and O(polylog N) depth, with controlled error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Block encoding is a core primitive in quantum algorithms (e.g., QSVT, Hamiltonian simulation, linear algebra). Semiseparable matrices arise widely in structured numerical linear algebra, but efficient quantum block encodings tailored to their generator representation were lacking.

Method: Construct an explicit circuit from the two generators of an N×N (N=2^n) semiseparable one-pair matrix. Building blocks are cyclic shifts, swaps, and Hadamard gates, each implemented efficiently via binary decomposition of indices. Uses 2n+2 ancilla qubits, depth O(polylog N), and state-preparation routines to load the generators.

Result: Realizes a (2√N, 2n+2, 2√2 ε)-block encoding, where ε is governed by the accuracy of the state-preparation routines loading the generators.

Conclusion: Provides a direct, depth-efficient quantum circuit for block-encoding generator-representable semiseparable matrices, avoiding full matrix materialization and enabling structured quantum linear-algebra routines.

Abstract: We present an explicit quantum circuit that constructs a block encoding of a generator-representable semiseparable (one-pair) matrix directly from its two generators. For an $N\times N$ semiseparable matrix with $N=2^{n}$, the circuit uses $2n+2$ ancilla qubits and has $O({\rm polylog} N)$ depth, and it realizes a $(2\sqrt{N},\,2n+2,\,2 \sqrt{2}\ \varepsilon)$-block encoding, where $\varepsilon$ is controlled by the accuracy of the state-preparation routines that load the generators. The building blocks of the circuit are cyclic shifts, swaps, and Hadamard gates, each admitting an efficient implementation through the binary decomposition of the relevant indices.

</details>


### [136] [Encoding Matters: A Qubit-Efficient Reformulation for Quantum Resilient Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13621)
*Emanuele G. Dalla Torre,Bat-El Friedman,Daniel Porat*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a structure-aware encoding reduction method to map logistics/routing problems onto fewer qubits, demonstrated on latency-resilient routing under dual-link failures, achieving 100X success probability improvement on trapped-ion hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Qubits double state space but also increase circuit size, constraint overhead, and noise exposure. For near-term quantum computers, reducing the required number of qubits is a central practical concern.

Method: A structure-aware encoding reduction method that efficiently maps logistics and routing problems onto qubits, demonstrated on a benchmark of latency-resilient routing under dual-link failures.

Result: The reduced formulation lowers both qubit count and compiled circuit complexity while preserving the original optimization task. Running on the same trapped-ion quantum computer yields a 100X improvement in success probability.

Conclusion: Careful problem formulation can make current quantum hardware substantially more effective for practical optimization.

Abstract: Qubits are powerful computational resources because each additional qubit doubles the size of the accessible state space. This expressive power comes at a cost: adding qubits also increases circuit size, constraint overhead, and exposure to hardware noise. These limitations are especially critical for near-term quantum computers, making the number of qubits required to formulate a problem a central practical concern. In this work, we present a method for efficiently mapping logistics and routing problems onto qubits through structure-aware encoding reduction. We demonstrate the method on a benchmark problem of latency-resilient routing under dual-link failures. For this benchmark, the reduced formulation substantially lowers both the qubit count and the compiled circuit complexity while preserving the original optimization task. By running the reduced model on the same trapped-ion quantum computer as the benchmark, we obtain a 100X improvement in the success probability. This finding shows how careful problem formulation can make current quantum hardware substantially more effective for practical optimization.

</details>

### [137] [Final-state centrality in relativistic heavy-ion simulations from neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13828)
*Anibal Bezerra,Fernando G. Gardim*

Main category: nucl-th

TL;DR: Neural networks trained on a few hundred hybrid events predict charged-particle multiplicity and mean transverse momentum with 1-2% error, enabling centrality estimation and fluctuation studies in heavy-ion collisions at low cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hydrodynamic simulations require millions of events to determine centrality from final-state quantities, which is impractical for hybrid models; centrality is thus approximated by initial entropy or energy, potentially biasing results.

Method: Train neural networks on a few hundred hybrid events to predict event-by-event multiplicity and mean transverse momentum [pT], then compare initial-state vs final-state centrality definitions and compute [pT] fluctuations and correlations.

Result: Predictions reach 1-2% relative error. For Pb-Pb at 2.76 TeV and Au-Au at 200 GeV, initial- and final-state definitions select nearly the same events in 10%-wide classes, but up to 20% change class at 1% width. [pT] correlation with initial-eccentricity elliptic flow proxy is insensitive to centrality definition; correlation with multiplicity changes substantially.

Conclusion: The approach enables cheap generation of millions of collisions and can be applied at every design point of Bayesian analyses with modest additional cost.

Abstract: Experiments in relativistic heavy-ion collisions determine centrality from final-state quantities, usually the charged-particle multiplicity. Performing the same in hydrodynamic simulations requires millions of events, which is impractical for state-of-the-art hybrid models, and centrality is therefore commonly approximated by the initial entropy or energy. We show that neural networks trained on a few hundred hybrid events predict the event-by-event multiplicity and mean transverse momentum $[p_T]$ with relative errors of 1--2\%, which makes it possible to generate millions of collisions at a small fraction of the cost of the full simulation. For Pb--Pb collisions at $2.76$~TeV and Au--Au collisions at $200$~GeV, the initial-state and final-state definitions select nearly the same events in 10\%-wide classes, but up to 20\% of the events change class when the classes are 1\% wide. We then compute the fluctuations of $[p_T]$ and its Pearson correlations with a proxy of the elliptic flow, estimated from the initial eccentricity, and with multiplicity. The first correlation is insensitive to how centrality is defined, while the second changes substantially. The approach can be applied at every design point of Bayesian analyses at modest additional cost.

</details>


### [138] [Final-state centrality in relativistic heavy-ion simulations from neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13828)
*Anibal Bezerra,Fernando G. Gardim*

Main category: nucl-th

TL;DR: Neural networks trained on a few hundred hybrid heavy-ion collision events can predict event-by-event multiplicity and mean transverse momentum with 1-2% relative error, enabling cheap generation of millions of events and accurate centrality determination for Bayesian analyses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Experimental centrality in relativistic heavy-ion collisions is defined from final-state quantities (usually charged multiplicity), but reproducing this in hydrodynamic simulations requires millions of events, which is impractical for state-of-the-art hybrid models. Centrality is therefore commonly approximated using the initial entropy or energy, potentially introducing biases.

Method: Neural networks are trained on a few hundred hybrid simulation events to predict event-by-event charged-particle multiplicity and mean transverse momentum [pT]. These predictions are used to generate millions of collisions cheaply and to compare initial-state versus final-state centrality definitions. Fluctuations of [pT] and its Pearson correlations with a proxy of elliptic flow (from initial eccentricity) and with multiplicity are then computed.

Result: The networks achieve 1-2% relative errors. For Pb-Pb at 2.76 TeV and Au-Au at 200 GeV, initial- and final-state centrality definitions select nearly the same events in 10%-wide classes, but up to 20% of events change class at 1% width. The [pT]-elliptic-flow-proxy correlation is insensitive to centrality definition, while the [pT]-multiplicity correlation changes substantially.

Conclusion: Neural-network emulation enables practical event-by-event centrality determination at a small fraction of full-simulation cost, and is applicable at every design point of Bayesian analyses with modest additional cost. Centrality definition strongly affects multiplicity-related fluctuation observables but not flow-related ones.

Abstract: Experiments in relativistic heavy-ion collisions determine centrality from final-state quantities, usually the charged-particle multiplicity. Performing the same in hydrodynamic simulations requires millions of events, which is impractical for state-of-the-art hybrid models, and centrality is therefore commonly approximated by the initial entropy or energy. We show that neural networks trained on a few hundred hybrid events predict the event-by-event multiplicity and mean transverse momentum $[p_T]$ with relative errors of 1--2\%, which makes it possible to generate millions of collisions at a small fraction of the cost of the full simulation. For Pb--Pb collisions at $2.76$~TeV and Au--Au collisions at $200$~GeV, the initial-state and final-state definitions select nearly the same events in 10\%-wide classes, but up to 20\% of the events change class when the classes are 1\% wide. We then compute the fluctuations of $[p_T]$ and its Pearson correlations with a proxy of the elliptic flow, estimated from the initial eccentricity, and with multiplicity. The first correlation is insensitive to how centrality is defined, while the second changes substantially. The approach can be applied at every design point of Bayesian analyses at modest additional cost.

</details>
